Ayrton Senna's Honda NSX Sells For Record $1.1 Million At Auction

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

One of the original NSX's biggest selling points was that Honda recruited three-time Formula 1 champion Ayrton Senna to help develop the sports car. While the legendary Brazilian wasn't impressed with the NSX at first, it evolved into a mass-produced machine he eventually praised. Senna's personal 1991 Honda NSX sold for $1.17 million at RM Sotheby's auction in London last week. The sale made the late champion's car the most expensive first-generation NSX ever sold at auction, surpassing a $1.06 million mark set for a 2003 NSX-R last year.

Honda was contractually obligated to provide Senna with an NSX for his personal use. The Japanese automaker supplied three examples of the sports car, a pair in black and one in Formula Red. The red NSX, the auctioned car, was stored in Portugal and delivered to Senna when he visited the country. It's the same car that the Brazilian was photographed washing with a garden hose. The 1992 documentary "Ayrton Senna: Racing Is In My Blood" also features Senna spinning the wheels on this NSX. After Senna's death in 1994, the sports car stayed with the Portuguese dealership that owned it until 1997.

Senna's most successful period in F1 came with McLaren from 1988 until 1993. The Brazilian won three F1 world championships and 35 races over the six seasons with the British team. Honda supplied engines to McLaren, ending the partnership after the 1992 season. If by fate, McLaren conducted a preseason test at Suzuka Circuit in February 1989, just as Honda's R&D department was wrapping up its month-long NSX testing program. The engineers let Senna run laps in the sports car. According to Road & Track, Senna wasn't pleased, stating, "I'm not sure I can really give you appropriate advice on a mass-production car, but I feel it's a little fragile."

Honda went back to the drawing board and increased the NSX's chassis stiffness by 50%. The automaker even invited Senna back to test the sports car at the Nürburgring and help tune the suspension. The expert input played a significant role in the NSX's success, from marketing to the sports car's critical success. While most of us didn't have the budget to bid on Senna's NSX, Honda is doing its part to help other NSX owners keep their cars just as pristine. The Japanese automaker's heritage program now produces over 3,000 official reproduction parts to keep first-generation NSXs on the road and running smoothly.

Free RPG WendyQuest Lets You Earn Real-Life Food Coupons

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

In a surprising turn of events, the popular fast food chain Wendy's has released an official pixelated RPG of its own, which takes heavy inspiration from classic turn-based JRPGs like Final Fantasy for its gameplay loop. Offered completely free on either Mobile or Steam, the WendyQuest RPG has about 10–15 hours of gameplay to offer.

While the charming art style and hardware requirements that could run on a "baked potato" according to its Steam page are already tempting, the real incentive for playing comes from the fact that defeating bosses will grant coupons that will earn you free food in real life, albeit with a few limitations.

As shared by Dexerto on X, by progressing through the game and defeating bosses with the power of your ever-growing party, each boss you defeat will offer an item that offers "a chance to win a different IRL Wendy's item" through the use of the Wendy's App, with each boss offering the following rewards:

Each of the free food items can also only be claimed as a bonus alongside another purchase, meaning you'll have to spend at least some money in order to actually claim your free food. The real-world coupons are also notably on a first-come, first-served basis, and once they run out, you won't be able to collect them anymore.

In the mood for a JRPG but lack the time needed to play one? Then these short, excellent JRPG experiences are exactly what you need!

While the surprisingly high quality of the game comes as a surprise, this isn't even the first time Wendy's has stepped into the gaming world, as the fast-food chain also released their own official Wendy's tabletop RPG, not to mention its custom Super Mario Maker campaign themed after the familiar mascot.

While this will likely be far from the last time Wendy's releases their own officially sponsored gaming product, there's no telling if the next one will be as generous with its free real-world offerings.

Passenger Forced To Pay ‘Employee Retention Fee’ For JFK Airport Self-Checkout Machines

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A traveler buying a $21.99 book at New York JFK used self-checkout but was still forced to pay an employee retention surcharge. Hudson’s Bryant Park Market in terminal 5 added the mandatory 3% fee, then sales tax on the fee. Does the machine’s employee wellness program cover maintenance on the cooling fan, or maybe paid time off to install Windows updates? The passenger bought John U. Bacon’s The Gales of November: The Untold Story of the Edmund Fitzgerald, and shared the self-checkout receipt. A 3% surcharge has been applied to your purchase. This charge is for employee and retention programs and is not a gratuity. Mandatory employee fee at bookstore when I used self checkout.by u/alternativo- in EndTipping The tax on the surcharge annoyed the shopper, too. But New York calculates sales tax on the full taxable purchase price. And the surcharge is just a hidden part of the price. I’ve written about airports allowing surcharges as an end-run around their own pricing rules. The Port Authority of New York New Jersey is clear on this. Its regulation generally caps prices at 15% above comparable street prices, but expressly allows an additional employee wage and benefit fee of up to 3%. The regulation also excludes the employee fee from gross receipts under agreements where the Port Authority collects a share of concession revenue. I covered the same 3% employee benefit and retention charge at a Newark self-checkout last year. The Port Authority tells merchants to add the fee, and incentivize them to do it – moving part of the price into fees means they get to keep more of the money. Philadelphia authorized a similar 3% add-on while retaining its street price cap. There it was expressly a way to let merchants charge passengers more and circumvent the cap. Minneapolis prefers a 4.5% hospitality charge with signage explaining it wasn’t a tip and didn’t go to the people providing the hospitality. The employee fee on this book isn’t a tip, but airport machines have been demanding those, too. In Austin, I couldn’t pay for a bottle of water without entering a tip. The terminal rejected $0.00. Staff confirmed it, and the transaction only went through when I entered a penny. Earlier this year I also flagged an Austin airport self-checkout that defaulted to an 18% tip while adding a card processing surcharge. One commenter asked about the JFK airport charge, “Since I would be paying the employee retention fee, do i get a say which employees stay or go?” I’d retain the scanner, but put whomever programmed the surcharge on a performance improvement plan. I’m So Obsessed With This Coffee Mug, I Tell Strangers About It

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

I wouldn't call myself a minimalist by any means, but I am not someone who buys a lot of kitchen gadgets or rushes to snag the latest seasonal cup. But ever since I picked up my first Fellow Carter Move Mug five years ago, I’ve happily repurchased it several times.

This sleek little mug, designed by the coffee nerds at Fellow, keeps your brew hot (or cold) without sacrificing flavor. I use it so often that I’ve sung its praises to a coffee shop owner in Washington, a bed-and-breakfast proprietor in Maine, and countless other strangers.

I love this mug more than I can express—so much, in fact, that I reached out to Fellow founder, Jake Miller, to chat about it. He launched the brand 13 years ago with the goal of delivering great design to coffee brewing, but customers kept asking for an exceptional way to drink their coffee on the go. So a company founded by and for coffee obsessives made the ultimate companion container.

The Carter Move Mug is designed with a superior drinking experience in mind. The body of the mug tapers into a thin drinking lip that Miller said was inspired by a wine glass. When you take a sip, you’re touching metal and not paint, and the lip is protected when the mug is closed.

At the time, other to-go cups were designed with a small opening in the lid and completely missed out on the aromatics. “It was a pretty opinionated design choice, but we wanted people to experience the incredible smells of coffee,” he explained. “Once the lid is off, you have this really great drinking experience.” While the lip of the mug is metal, the inside of the mug is ceramic-coated, so it doesn’t impart any taste to the coffee. According to Miller, “that allows for a more pure expression of the coffee.”

When I tell people about the Carter Mug, I like to joke that it’s almost too good at its job. Hot drinks stay very hot for hours, and I regularly find ice in the mug a full day after I first made my chilled version. I once left a mug of hot coffee in a car during a blizzard, and after almost a day, the coffee was still pleasant to drink. Miller said this effective temperature control is from two things: the distance between the two walls and how much vacuum insulation the mug has. Also, Fellow designed the lid to be gasket-tight so there’s minimal heat loss.

One of my favorite features of the Move Mug is the splash guard that clicks into the vessel and prevents big spills while you’re drinking in motion. (As a person prone to mess, this is very helpful.) This element, along with some other accessories, was born out of feedback based on how customers use their mugs, including a straw for cold coffee, a cup holder-friendly size, and the option for a slide top lid. “Everything we do is customer-driven,” Miller noted.

Maybe not important to how your coffee tastes — but important to aesthetics — the Move Mug is sleek and sophisticated, which means it’s suitable for most situations. There’s a nice range of colors, and it looks great in my hand on my commute and on my desk in the office. It’s truly the perfect accessory.

South Korea wants to delay its 22% crypto tax again and give investors a new break on losses

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The government now backs a 2029 start and a three-year loss carryforward, while the existing January 2027 timetable remains in place pending legislation.

South Korea’s government said on Nov. 5 it would pursue a two-year postponement of crypto income taxation to 2029, alongside a proposed three-year loss carryforward, in consultation with the National Assembly.

The announcement follows the Democratic Party’s Nov. 4 request for another delay, less than two months before the scheduled January 2027 start.

The government’s plan still requires legislation. National Tax Service guidance continues to specify taxation of eligible crypto transfers and lending from Jan. 1, 2027.

Party leader Kim Min-seok said the party wanted the Digital Asset Basic Act to establish an overall framework before giving companies and investors an adjustment period, Yonhap reported. He also identified overseas transaction information, computer-system verification and taxpayer guidance as preparations that should accompany another postponement.

The government cited expected crypto transaction-information exchange with the United States from 2029 and changes anticipated under digital-asset legislation as reasons for seeking two more years of preparation. It also proposed allowing losses to be carried forward for three years, so they could offset later gains when calculating taxable income.

Kim’s Nov. 4 remarks reported by Yonhap specified no replacement date, although the agency said committee members had mainly discussed two years. The government’s subsequent announcement now identifies 2029 as its target.

A 2029 start already appears in a legislative proposal. An Aug. 28 release from Rep. Kim Sang-hoon describes his Income Tax Act amendment to move commencement from Jan. 1, 2027 to Jan. 1, 2029. That proposed date remains conditional on an enacted change to the timetable.

Qualitative status map using the current NTS schedule and the government’s November 5 proposal. Solid and dashed connectors distinguish the existing schedule from the change requiring legislation; lengths do not measure time or probability.

Status as of Nov 5, 2026. The proposed 2029 date is not enacted; the graphic does not forecast passage.

The Democratic Party previously agreed to a two-year moratorium in December 2024. The NTS says an amendment passed that month produced the January 2027 timetable now facing investors.

Under the NTS framework for residents, crypto income comes from transfers and lending after acquisition costs and associated expenses are deducted. An annual 2.5 million won basic deduction applies, followed by a 20% national income-tax rate. The calculation therefore concerns income after costs and the deduction, rather than the total value of an investor’s holdings.

The combined rate is 22% when local income tax is included, as separately described in Kim Sang-hoon’s parliamentary release.

For crypto held before Jan. 1, 2027, the NTS uses the higher of actual acquisition cost and the asset’s 2026 year-end market value as the starting cost basis. Residents net annual gains and losses and report the income in May of the following year.

The 2027 dates describe the current scheduled regime. Moving the start to 2029 and introducing the proposed loss relief will require legislative changes.

Scott Parker has been sent £18m West Ham message and Spurs are laughing loudest

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Scott Parker’s start to life as West Ham head coach got off to a thrilling start.

The Hammers drew 3-3 against Cardiff City on Wednesday night in his first game in the dugout, with the team coming from 3-0 behind to secure a point on the road.

Goals from Divine Mukasa, Arne Engels and Max Kilman sealed an exciting comeback in the second half, with the London outfit hoping that they can compete for automatic promotion this season.

But Parker’s first game as manager showed that there is perhaps more work to be done at the London Stadium than initially thought.

The first issue that Parker may have to address as the new West Ham boss is the underwhelming form of summer arrival Manor Solomon.

The winger was brought in with the expectation that he could power the team to promotion, much like he did at Leeds United in 2024/25, where he contributed 10 goals and 12 assists in 39 Championship appearances.

Should Scott Parker drop Manor Solomon? Comment your opinion below

But his form has been inconsistent at the London Stadium so far, and another underwhelming performance on Wednesday night led to him being hauled off after 69 minutes.

While he managed to complete 13 of his 15 attempted passes, the overall total matches that of someone quite uninvolved in the game, and he also failed to create a chance or take a clear shot at goal, leading to a deserved substitution (all stats from Fotmob).

Edvin Austbo was chosen to replace Solomon for the final 20 minutes against Cardiff, making his 10th appearance in the league this season.

This was the eighth time he came off the bench, and his lively performance helped the team in their comeback from 3-0 down.

West Ham United considered the potential appointment of former Tottenham Hotspur and Liverpool coach Johnny Heitinga before deciding on Scott Parker

The Norwegian already has as many goals and assists as Solomon so far this campaign, despite playing only 347 minutes compared to 1,003.

The 21-year-old arrived from Viking IF in a deal worth £18 million in the summer, and now could be the time for Parker to give him an extended run in the team ahead of Solomon.

Austbo hasn’t had an extended run in the team, which impacts how we can judge him, but his appearances so far have shown a lot of promise.

The winger has an expected assists (xA) per 90 of 0.53, creating 0.53 big chances per 90.

He also has 1.32 successful crosses each game at a success rate of 35.7 per cent, which makes him a dangerous threat in the final third, all while also taking 2.63 shots each game himself.

If he can replicate these kinds of performances over a series of starts in the coming weeks, then Parker will have no choice but to keep him in the lineup going forward over Solomon.

West Ham’s rivals Tottenham Hotspur signed Solomon in 2023, but he struggled for game time under various managers at the London outfit, which led to loan spells at Leeds, Villarreal and Fiorentina.

But the Hammers committed to a £5 million deal to sign the winger despite his lack of impact at Spurs.

Given the question marks over his quality now, the Premier League side have had the last laugh on their rival.

Parker dropping him to the bench would be the ultimate vindication for Spurs’ decision to cash in on the forward last summer.

Tech Giants Are Putting Billions Behind a Decades-Old Engineering Model. Here’s Why It Matters More Now.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

The FDE title might be new, but the theory is as old as computers themselves.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

If you’ve followed tech news this summer, you’ve almost certainly come across the term Forward Deployed Engineer or, in short, FDE. On June 30, AWS announced a massive $1 billion investment in a new unit built around this one role. OpenAI had already put more than $4 billion behind its own Deployment Company in May. There’s no end to the news on FDE. All of this for just one role.

So, who are they? A forward deployed engineer is an engineer embedded directly in a customer’s environment to design, build and ship AI or custom software solutions. Palantir, which made the title popular, describes the difference well. A regular engineer builds “one capability, many customers.” An FDE delivers “one customer, many capabilities.”

Companies have put engineers close to customers for decades. In the 1950s and 60s, IBM sent systems engineers to sit with customers alongside its mainframes, writing code and configuring the machine — all of it included in the price of the hardware until IBM started charging for it separately at the end of the 60s. Later, consultants embedded themselves inside client organizations, and implementation teams worked side by side with business users.

Because the way enterprise software gets built has changed, and the FDE is the role that fits the new way of building.

Across years, the best way to describe the business and IT relationship was like a client and vendor. Business teams gave the requirement. IT took that requirement and took six months, sometimes a year, with large teams across development, integration, testing and training for a project.

A lot got lost along the way. What the business wrote and what IT understood were rarely the same thing, and that gap only showed up in testing, the most expensive place to find it. And because IT built it and IT ran it, the business never really owned it. Every small change went back into the queue. This is the gap between a solution being “deployed” and being “valuable.”

But now, with AI-assisted coding, or vibe coding, a business user can put together a first working version of an app in days. It won’t be production-ready, but it’s real enough to show what they mean instead of describing it in a document.

That changes the order of things. The process doesn’t have to be frozen before you automate it. You can reimagine the process while you build, because trying an idea now costs a few days, not a quarter.

But this first-version app lacks the practical connect to the data or the information that another team has. It doesn’t pass a security review. It doesn’t scale. Someone has to take what the business already knows it wants and turn it into something the enterprise can actually run.

So, the hard part is no longer just building the technology. It is getting technology, data, workflows and people to work together in a way that changes an outcome. That too, quickly. That requires a different form of accountability.

A true FDE is an engineer first. They write production code, they work inside the customer’s data and systems, and they ship. Someone who writes a strategy deck under a new title is a consultant.

Engineering depth or technical depth: SQL, cloud, Python and real experience putting LLMs and agents into production. They need to understand the ins and outs of governance, security and compliance.

Functional knowledge: Doesn’t mean you need doctors to work in pharma and life sciences projects. For example, OpenAI’s healthcare FDE role asks for experience with payer workflows, provider operations, EHR systems or standards like HL7 and FHIR, and the ability to “translate payer, provider or health-system workflows into technical requirements and measurable outcomes.” The same posting says clinical credentials are not required.

So functional knowledge here means three things. Knowing how work actually moves through the function: who approves what, where the handoffs are, where it breaks. Knowing the systems and data that function runs on. And knowing the rules it operates under. An FDE in healthcare billing needs different context from one in supply chain. What they don’t need is to be the subject matter expert. They need to sit with one and ask the right questions.

Discovery and judgment: As per my experience with FDEs, they’re spending roughly 60%-70% of their time with the customer. They need to scope problems that come with no spec and explain technical trade-offs in business terms.

Outcome ownership: The roles that pay a premium are tied to customer outcomes, not tickets closed. Let’s go back to the IBM systems engineer era. The outcome there was “Did we deploy the system successfully?” and that’s it. Now it has changed to “Did the system create measurable value?” — essentially owning the business outcome.

And the team is lean. Inside an enterprise, the way we’d staff it is two or three engineers, someone from the business who knows the function inside out, someone accountable for data and the platform, and a business owner who holds the metric.

This is the question I get most from CIOs. If a small team is building fast with the business, what stops it from becoming shadow IT with better branding?

A true FDE team works inside enterprise standards, not around them:

This is the governance you already have, applied while you build instead of after.

For us at Polestar Analytics, this is not just what we preach but what we follow. We have forward deployed marketers in industry teams, for example, who get visibility into what the need of the hour is, right where the need is. And many more.

Simply put, the FDE title might be new, but the theory is as old as computers themselves. What’s changed is that the engineer now sits with the business, builds with the business and owns the outcome with the business.

If you’ve followed tech news this summer, you’ve almost certainly come across the term Forward Deployed Engineer or, in short, FDE. On June 30, AWS announced a massive $1 billion investment in a new unit built around this one role. OpenAI had already put more than $4 billion behind its own Deployment Company in May. There’s no end to the news on FDE. All of this for just one role.

So, who are they? A forward deployed engineer is an engineer embedded directly in a customer’s environment to design, build and ship AI or custom software solutions. Palantir, which made the title popular, describes the difference well. A regular engineer builds “one capability, many customers.” An FDE delivers “one customer, many capabilities.”

We'll be in your inbox every morning Monday-Saturday with all the day’s top business news, inspiring stories, best advice and exclusive reporting from Entrepreneur.

Talking Point: How Often Do You Buy The Games Leaving Xbox Game Pass?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Once a game is marked for removal from Xbox Game Pass, how often do you buy it? The thought crossed my mind whilst I was putting together our recent roundup of November leavers, and it made me curious to hear the Pure Xbox community's thoughts.

On November 15th, 1000xRESIST will vanish from all tiers of Xbox Game Pass. That means if I want to play it - and I believe I do - then I'll have to buy it. As is customary with games marked for removal, Xbox usually slaps on a discount until the deed is done and the game is gone. 20% unless it's been featured in a sale elsewhere.

That makes 1000xRESIST a £13.39 / $15.99 game, knocking a whopping £3.35 / $4.00 off its full price. It's a fairly cheap game, but that's not much of a discount in the grand scheme of things. Considering I'm short on time to play it before it leaves Xbox Game Pass, and I'm still unsure whether it'll be my kind of game, I'd like to see a more convincing discount before pulling the trigger.

Xbox isn't shy of a good sale, either. I should know: I spend a fair amount of time rummaging through them, hoping to find you the best deals. In my experience, 20% is about 20% off the sweet spot for a sale price. It's a tough sell for games that have been lingering in your backlog for the year-or-so they've spent on Xbox Game Pass for free (assuming we're counting XGP as girl math).

Thinking about it, I've maybe bought two or three games between the moment they're nominated as a leaver and the day they actually leave Xbox Game Pass. What typically happens is that I gamble and wait for the game to go on sale good and proper. That, or I cave and fork out more money than its leaving discount would have cost me.

So, I'd like to put the question to you: how often do you buy the games leaving Xbox Game Pass? If it's something you do regularly, then great! Tell us which games you've bought and why. If not, how come? I'd also love a chat about what you do once a game is announced as a gonner. Do you rush to play it? Can you start a game knowing you don't have enough time to finish it? I want to know!

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Yellow Veil Pictures has boarded North American distribution rights for Callum Devlin‘s “The Weed Eaters,” a New Zealand cannibal horror film that notched a best feature win at SXSW Sydney in 2025. The indie banner is planning a winter release.

The deal for “The Weed Eaters” comes after the independent feature first debuted in the Unreleased Gems section of the Letterboxd Video Store, a video-on-demand streaming platform launched by the film-focused social media platform in 2025.

Why tech stocks are down today, including Nvidia, SpaceX, AMD, more

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

After a huge stock market rally on Wednesday following the decisive Democratic win in the U.S. midterms, stocks tumbled sharply on Thursday as investors feared a slowdown in data center construction and the AI industry as a whole.

The Dow Jones Industrial Average dropped 1,600 points on Thursday, about 3 percent of its value, according to CNBC. The S&P 500, a stock market index of large companies, and the tech-centered Nasdaq index, also dived on Thursday, with the latter dropping approximately 5 percent, the New York Times reported.

The massive drops in the stock market indexes even stoked fears of a Wall Street trading freeze. The S&P 500 drop reached 3.8 percent at one point on Thursday; at 7 percent, a market-mandated “circuit breaker” kicks in and forces a pause on all trading for 15 minutes.

The circuit breakers are intended to halt severe market crashes. A 13 percent drop in the S&P 500 leads to another forced freeze on trading, while a 20 percent crash ends trading for the day.

Chipmakers like Nvidia and Qualcomm took a big hit on Wall Street, as investors anticipated more regulation of the AI industry from Democratic legislators taking power in January. Goodbye from Salon

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Today, after 31 years, we are saddened to announce that Salon is ceasing publication.

Itâs difficult to find the right words for the end of something that has meant so much to so many people. Salon was never just a website. It was an idea about what journalism could be, born at a moment when the internet was still a largely unexplored frontier.

When Salon launched in November 1995, the web was a very different place. A small group of journalists set out to create something original: a publication with a strong voice, a curiosity about the world and a belief that the internet could bring journalism and readers together in new ways. They helped pioneer a form of journalism that would come to define an entirely new era of publishing.

Over three decades, Salon has brought together politics, culture, criticism and ideas in a way that was distinctly our own. Our journalism examined power and politics, questioned assumptions, surfaced stories that deserved attention and made room for perspectives that were too often overlooked. Our writers and editors helped shape conversations about the forces changing our lives. The work was never simply about keeping up with the news. It was about making sense of it.

The history of Salon is also the history of the people who made it. We are profoundly grateful to the journalists, editors, photographers, producers, designers, business teams and countless other colleagues who brought this publication to life across its many chapters. To the founders who imagined it, the staff members who built it, the contributors who gave it their voices and the alumni who carried its spirit into the world: Thank you. Salon’s legacy belongs to all of you.

The business realities of independent digital journalism have changed profoundly over the years. After careful consideration, we have reached the difficult decision that our publishing model is no longer sustainable. We know this news will be deeply disappointing to many of you, and we share that sadness.

Thirty-one years is a remarkable run, but what matters most is what happened during those years: the journalism, the ideas, the voices, the conversations and the community that grew around them. Salon helped demonstrate what digital journalism could be and could become. We hope that legacy will continue to matter long after today.

Thank you for being part of our story. It has been an extraordinary privilege.

Democrats Will Focus on Affordability in 2027, Party Leaders Say

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Democrats rode affordability anxieties to big wins in the midterm elections. But they must deliver on lowering costs if they want to sustain their political power, Senate Democratic Leader Chuck Schumer (D-NY) said Thursday.

Speaking at a Washington, DC, forum hosted by Center for American Progress, a left-leaning think tank, Schumer reflected on the significant gains in Tuesday's midterm elections. Democrats were able to flip dozens of seats and gain control of both the House and Senate for the upcoming Congress.

Democrats spent the year attacking President Donald Trump on "costs, chaos, and corruption," as Schumer put it. But although many people voted for Democrats out of frustration with the Trump administration, that doesn't mean they're enthusiastic about the Democratic Party, he conceded.

"We highlighted how Trump's chaos agenda was causing Americans pain at the grocery store, at the gas pump," Schumer said. "Every month in the spring, we focused on the costs of the American people and what we were going to do about it."

Vice President JD Vance has also said that the conflict in Iran and subsequent inflation surge cost Republicans the election. Schumer said Democrats have to bring down costs. Ending the Iran conflict and stopping the tariffs policies that have driven up costs in the country are two ways to do that.

Although Democrats made major gains, they still won't have enough votes to override a Trump veto. And Democratic leadership will have to find common ground with the party's left flank after a wave of Democratic Socialist candidates won elections on Tuesday, a fact Schumer acknowledged without mentioning the group by name.

"Unity means linking arms with people you will not agree with on every issue so you can make progress on the more important areas where you agree," Schumer said. "Intra-party debate isn't a sign of weakness."

Democrats who won state and local races have promised affordability-minded reforms and emphasized housing production. That's crucial in expensive areas such as California and Washington DC.

Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker said at the CAP event that medical and student debt are also issues Democrats should tackle at the federal level. These debts significantly delay homeownership. But he said both oversight and affordability should be Congress' main focus next year.

"You can walk and chew gum, we have to deal with affordability, that has to be the principal thing," Pritzer said. "But we have to have oversight committees, just like the Republicans did."

On Wednesday, New York Rep. Hakeem Jeffries, who is likely to become the new speaker of the House, said the House Democratic Caucus will convene next week and lay out an agenda. But Democrats would focus on "affordability and accountability" with their new majority, he said.

Democrats after the election threw out a host of ideas for items to pursue in 2027 and 2028. Many of them focused on both affordability and guardrails for artificial intelligence and data centers.

"Top of the Democratic agenda on day 1 needs to be a pause on advanced AI development, regulation of this out of control industry, and a ban on artificial superintelligence," Rep. Yassamin Ansari (D-AZ) posted to X on Wednesday.

The Nasdaq plunged 5% the next day, led by a sharp decline in tech stocks.

Sen. Bernie Sanders (I-VT), who caucuses with the Democrats, called for a national 5% wealth tax in a video message Thursday morning. Such a tax, as is being considered in California, could fund 7 million units of affordable housing, Sanders said.

Realtor.com® economists quantified just how bad the affordability situation is for Americans.

In January, a $2,000 monthly mortgage payment supported a home price $28,500 higher than a year earlier. By October 2026, that same payment supported a home price $33,600 lower. The surge in interest rates, from 6.05% in February to 7.33% in October, was to blame for the $62,100 swing.

“Mortgage rates are not abstract to a family trying to buy a home,” says Danielle Hale, chief economist at Realtor.com. “They show up as the home they can no longer afford."

"The year-over-year comparison is a vivid measure of why housing affordability lands as a pocketbook issue for voters," Hale continues. "The conflict’s inflationary impact is one source of uncertainty, but households feel the consequences in their monthly budgets and in the homes they have to take off their list.”

In a post, National Association of Homebuilders Chief Advocacy Officer Ken Wingert urged the next Congress to craft more housing bills like the comprehensive 21st Century Road to Housing Act that passed this summer.

Lawmakers have suggested they have their own sets of ideas for new housing measures to pursue. Cities and localities, they have said, must also step up to implement new laws.

Those rules must assuage builders whose confidence has been shattered by high costs of building materials and high mortgage rates chasing homebuyers out of the market. The National Association of Home Builders/Wells Fargo Housing Market Index (HMI) fell to a 14-year low last month.

"With a national housing shortfall of roughly 1.2 million households, more must be done to let builders build—and voters agree," Wingert said. "Throughout the campaign, voters consistently cited housing availability and affordability as key factors in their decisions."

Tristan Navera is a senior reporter on housing policy, covering trends and solutions in the housing market from Washington, DC. He was previously a senior reporter at Bloomberg Law, and before that covered real estate for the Washington Business Journal. Earlier in his career, he spent a decade reporting on business and real estate in Dayton and Columbus, OH. A Cincinnati native, he holds a journalism degree from Ohio University.

A teen used her school Chromebook and Google AI Mode to plan her suicide, lawsuit alleges

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

A family is suing Google for alleged negligence in offering Gemini to student learners via school-issued Chromebooks, after the AI chatbot aided their teen daughter in planning her suicide.

The Chromebooks offered dangerous chatbot access, the lawsuit alleges.

The teen, referred to in the lawsuit as Emma, did not go through with the drafted plan. Her family was alerted to the concerning behavior by a former school staffer. Emma's mother was then able to observe discussion of suicide with the chatbot in real-time, the family claims.

According to the lawsuit, filed by the EdTech Law Center and Qureshi Law in California Superior Court this week, both Google and the school's third-party monitoring software failed to flag or warn Emma's parents about her chatbot use.

"Google has no right to use its stranglehold on public schools to force its dangerous Gemini chatbot on a captive audience of millions of kids," said EdTech Law Center founder Julie Liddell in a press release.

Emma was reportedly able to access Gemini, which is restricted to minors, via Google Search's AI Mode on her school Chromebook. The lawsuit states she began interacting with the chatbot intensely in December 2025, becoming convinced she had a host of psychological disorders. Gemini instructed her on how to conceal her self-harm, responded to questions about suicide methods, and provided help writing a eulogy, the lawsuit alleges.

"This is the sick new reality Google has created by forcing its AI products onto school-issued devices," Brendan Bouffard, Associate General Counsel for AI and Privacy at child safety nonprofit Fairplay, said in a statement. "Google claims its Chromebooks are there to help students learn, but in reality, the company was experimenting on kids like Emma by pushing them to use Gemini, a product the company knew was dangerous but rushed out anyway."

The lawsuit names Google CEO Sundar Pichai and cofounder Sergey Brin specifically, and accuses the company of "defective design, failure to warn, negligence, fraudulent concealment, and infliction of emotional distress." The family also claims the product violates California’s new Companion Chatbot Law. A Google spokesperson told the New York Times that the company was reviewing the claims but had worked "extensively" with health experts to design features and emergency resources in the event of a mental health crisis.

Child safety groups — including parent-led coalitions in school districts nationwide — have advocated for AI moratoriums, which range from multi-year bans on all generative AI tools to age-based restrictions on AI and screens. Los Angeles and New York City public schools announced sweeping restrictions on AI use for the 2026-2027 school year following intense pressure from parent groups, and other states are following suit.

States have taken the lead on regulating minor access to chatbots. In September, California Gov. Gavin Newsom announced a myriad of new child safety protections, including "Adam's Law," named after Adam Raine, a teen who died by suicide after consulting with ChatGPT. Just last week, Gov. Kathy Hochul unveiled a new chatbot safety law for the state of New York, imposing some of the strongest restrictions on minor chatbot use in the nation. Beyond state-level restrictions, many groups are spearheading federal legislation like the People-First Chatbot Bill and the GUARD Act.

The debate around chatbot safety and whether AI is beneficial to young minds is coming to a head in schools.

Earlier this week, Google announced a revision to its classroom technology policies, turning off access to its AI tools — including Gemini, Gemini Notebook, and Gemini in Classroom — by default for all education accounts.

Under the new policy, schools will have to actively opt-in to AI features in order for students and staff to access such tools on school devices. Because Google Search now has, and doesn't offer a version of its Search browser without Gemini, schools with AI bans would also be forced to choose between permitting access to Search and violating their own anti-AI guidelines.

A July report by safety nonprofit Common Sense Media found that Google Search's AI Overviews and AI Mode posed an "unacceptable risk" to young Googlers. Education advocates told Mashable at the time that Search was a "backdoor" to the company's Gemini model, and warned that continued access to the search engine without proper guardrails could pose risks for young learners.

"Let this be a reminder that ‘Google’ is not a verb," wrote Bouffard. "It’s the name of a surveillance and advertising company whose presence in schools has nothing to do with learning and everything to do with turning our kids into lifelong loyal customers."

Asteroids' Oddball Shapes Make Deflecting Them Challenging

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

If you're looking for uniformity in Nature, you won't find it in the asteroid belt. Not only are asteroid compositions different because they come from different parent bodies, but their shapes are different, too. They're not massive enough to round themselves like planets do.

This is more than just another space factoid; asteroid shapes matter. It's a near-certainty, given enough time, that a dangerous asteroid will head directly for a collision with Earth, and that's something scientists are actively studying. They want to develop ways to knock an asteroid off course, if necessary. That's what NASA's Double Asteroid Redirection Test (DART), and the ESA's follow-up Hera mission were about.

There are multiple concepts for redirecting a dangerous asteroid. One of them is a nuclear weapon. Not to obliterate an asteroid, but to bombard it with radiation by exploding the nuclear weapon in close proximity to the asteroid. This is called a stand-off X-ray mitigation scenario. The radiation pulse will change an asteroid's course, if done soon enough and done correctly.

But the outcome of the pulse needs to be predictable to work, and that's where asteroids' different shapes come into play.

"The effectiveness of deflecting Near Earth Objects (NEOs) using pulsed radiation is challenging to quantify because it hinges critically on surface topography, which is often unknown," write the authors of new research in Icarus. The research is "Efficient computation of vapor ejecta over asteroid geometries during pulsed energy deflection," and the lead author is Nathan Moore. Moore is a physicist at Sandia National Laboratories.

A simple kinetic impactor is probably the preferred choice for deflecting an asteroid. That's what the DART mission did, and it appeared to be successful. Though it only changed the velocity of its target Dimorphos by a little, it was done soon enough to create a substantial change in its position over years.

But the more pertinent issue concerns an asteroid, or perhaps a comet, that's on a collision course with Earth, but isn't discovered soon enough for an impactor to effectively change its trajectory.

"While considered an option of last resort, it has been argued that late-time interception of a near-Earth object (NEO) or comet, either for disruption or deflection, might only be possible with the higher energy concentration available from detonation of a pulsed energy device," the authors write. 'Pulsed energy device' is one way of saying nuclear weapon. In space, there's no atmospheric shock wave from a nuclear explosion, only radiation, which is released in a powerful pulse.

"Stand-off energy burstsâthose detonated far from the surfaceâhold a potential advantage compared to surface explosions in that they might permit deflection with less disruption to the original structure, allowing for more controlled steering of the asteroid and its debris," the authors write. Disrupting the original structure might make for a good action sequence in a movie, but it would multiply the hazard to Earth by creating a swarm of debris, some of which could strike our planet.

Researchers have simulated using nuclear weapons and their radiation pulses to deflect asteroids, but their irregular shapes have stymied them. These shapes are far more difficult to model, so most research has focused on spherical asteroids. Moore and his colleagues developed a new algorithm to overcome these limitations.

âOur new algorithm is over 1,600 times faster, which was critical to this breakthrough,â Moore said. âWith the old models, computers just couldnât handle it.â

In this research, the authors simulated known asteroids with more irregular shapes, an important improvement on previous work.

"The approach improves on earlier methods by accounting for the actual topography of the asteroid fully in three dimensions without unacceptably increasing computational cost or reducing accuracy," the authors explain. "As a demonstration, we predict the deflection of three bodies of different shapeâasteroid (101955) Bennu, asteroid (25143) Itokawa, and asteroid (6489) Golevkaâfrom both near-field and far-field irradiations."

These images of asteroids Itokawa (left) and Bennu (middle), and a computer model of asteroid Golevka (right), show how asteroid shapes can be very different from one another. Image Credits: Itokawa: By ESO, CC BY 4.0, https://commons.wikimedia.org/w/index.php?curid=170826264. Bennu: By NASA/Goddard/University of Arizona - Public Domain. Golevka: By NASA/JPL - NASA - Jet Propulsion Laboratory. Public Domain.

A sequence of events unfolds after a nuclear device is detonated near an asteroid. First, an intense x-ray pulse is directed at one side of the asteroid. Then there's rapid surface vaporization, and that creates most of the deflection thrust. Next is an internal shock wave, a powerful blow that travels through the asteroid's body. Finally, the internal structure of the asteroid is fractured, and that can blow chunks of the asteroid off the surface opposite the blast, and off of the asteroid's sides.

So despite there being no atmospheric shock wave, the asteroid is still disrupted.

The results highlighted how important an asteroid's shape is. Depending on which side of the asteroid was facing the radiation blast, the deflection velocity can be up to five times greater. "We find that the orientation of the body at time of intercept can increase the deflection velocity by up to nearly 2Ã for Bennu, 4Ã for Itokawa, and 5Ã for Golevka, compared to the minimum response for each body," the authors write.

This figure shows the pressure distribution as averaged over the first 20 Î¼s of vapor expansion, for asteroid (a) Bennu, (b) Itokawa, and (c) Golevka, for near and far Height Of Bursts (HOB). The vaporization is what creates most of the deflection thrust. Image Credit: Moore et al. 2026. Icarus.

âWe expected aim to matter,â said lead author Moore in a press release. âWhat surprised us was how much.â

The simulation in this work can be used on any asteroid that's been mapped by a spacecraft during a flyby. The size of the explosion, the distance, and the surface exposed to it can all be calculated in advance. By calculating the surface vaporization, the main factor in deflection, the simulation determines how hard the asteroid will be pushed, how fast it will rotate, and how much of the surface might break up.

The interior structure of asteroids can be quite different, and that's beyond the capabilities of this model to account for. "Right now, our computer model only considers breakup of rocks on the surface. We canât predict breakup of the entire asteroid body accurately yet," Moore said.

Real life is always different from simulations, and that means that these results can't tell us with absolute certainty what will happen. But it's a step forward, one that drives home how critical the aim is to a successful asteroid deflection, should one be needed in the future.

"The strong sensitivity of deflection and disruption to asteroid topography is especially pertinent given that the shape of an incoming NEO might not be known, and its orientation at time of intercept may be uncontrolled," the authors write. Their work as it stands now can at least be used to make estimates, which is better, far better, than nothing.

"Alternatively, effort could be made to obtain high-resolution topographic characterization of an incoming NEO to improve accuracy of the deflection model, such as using spacecraft-based imagery or altimetry acquired before detonation," the authors explain.

It's possible that asteroid-mapping spacecraft could stand at the ready in space, waiting to be deployed. They could even be paired with asteroid-disrupting spacecraft carrying nuclear weapons. Or the two capabilities could be combined into one spacecraft. Placing atomic weapons in space would be extremely tricky in our fractured geopolitical world, but it's still a possibility, maybe the only possibility.

But however we prepare for an incoming asteroid, this work highlights how important it is that our aim is true.

âOur study shows how important it is to aim carefully,â Moore said. âPick the right side and you win. Guess wrong and it could be a bad day.â

Riskier decisions may offer an early warning of heavier drinking

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Changes in phone game decisions predicted heavier drinking the following month in a yearlong study of people with alcohol use disorder.

Alcohol has a reputation for making people take risks. But a yearlong study of people with alcohol use disorder found that changes in decision making came before heavier drinking.

When participants took certain risks in phone games, they drank more the following month. Heavier drinking, however, did not predict riskier choices the next month.

The researchers tracked changes within each person rather than simply comparing heavier and lighter drinkers.

Most participants had mild or moderate alcohol use disorder, making them a potentially important group for early intervention.

Hilmar Zech, a postdoctoral researcher at University Hospital Würzburg and TU Dresden in Germany, led the study with Lorenz Deserno.

The team also included co-authors at Charité in Berlin and the Central Institute of Mental Health.

“This temporal direction was specific and was not observed vice versa, even after accounting for the amount of previous drinking,” Deserno said.

The researchers followed 603 people, ages 16 to 65, for one year. All of them met at least two of diagnostic criteria for alcohol use disorder.

In the study, a standard drink held 0.4 ounces (12 grams) of pure alcohol. The participants averaged about three of those a day, and about five on the days they drank.

Every two days, each person reported what they had drunk from a list of 19 options, from a small beer to a bottle of wine.

Every four weeks, they also played four five-minute games on their phones. In one game, fruit fell from a tree and the player tapped to collect it, unless the fruit turned brown.

In another, red circles flashed on a grid and the player had to remember their positions. Those two measured self-control.

The other two measured decision making. In a gambling game, the player chose between a sure amount and a coin flip, in versions built around wins, losses, or both.

In a card game, the player could pay to flip over cards before guessing which of two face-down rows added up to more.

The mixed version offered a possible win or a possible loss on the same flip. In months when a person took that gamble more often, that person drank more the next month.

The card game gave the same result. When someone flipped fewer cards before guessing, they drank more in the month that followed.

In the loss version, the link was reversed. In months when a person gambled more to avoid a sure loss, they drank less the following month.

Earlier studies had compared people with alcohol use disorder with people without it at a single point in time.

Those studies found a similar pattern: people with alcohol use disorder were more willing to gamble when both winning and losing were possible, but less willing when the gamble involved only potential losses.

As far as Zech’s team knows, no study had watched those shifts come and go inside one person and then watched the drinking follow.

“We found that monthly fluctuations in aspects of decision making predicted subsequent alcohol consumption,” Zech said.

Next the team ran the question backward. Did heavy drinking in one month change how a person played the games the next month?

The researchers found no evidence of that pattern.

The forward link also held after the team accounted for the fact that one month’s drinking resembles the last.

In a second analysis built for a year with many zero-drink days, the loss-version result held up, and the other two showed the same direction with more uncertainty.

The two self-control games did not follow that pattern.

A person’s monthly ups and downs in avoiding rotten fruit or remembering circles had no link to the drinking that followed.

Working memory still mattered, but between people instead of within one person.

Participants who could hold more circles in mind drank less on average over the year than participants who could hold fewer.

Cameron Carter, editor-in-chief of the journal that published the study, explained why experimental tasks matter.

“Decision making and cognitive control cannot be accurately examined through interviews or self-reports and instead require experimental tasks,” Carter said.

The effects were small. Zech’s team wrote that they are likely too small to be useful in a clinic yet.

The team had also planned to split the sample in two, with the second half meant to confirm what the first half found.

The pattern was clear in the first half and not in the second, although the two halves did not differ from each other when the team compared them directly.

The team also split the 603 people into random halves 1,000 times. The direction came out the same every time, and a group of about 300 was often too small to detect the effect.

Of the 603 who started, 218 dropped out early. Their data stayed in, and leaving was not tied to how they played or how much they drank.

The self-control games may simply have been played too rarely. A few studies that checked several times a day did find self-control dipping before drinking, though only under certain conditions.

Once a month may be too rare to catch that. And a study that watches people without changing anything can’t say that the risky choices caused the drinking.

Whether a game score can predict heavier drinking over the following week, rather than month, is still an open question.

The team envisions an app that flags changes in a person’s risk-taking score and offers automated support or prompts them to seek help in person.

Whether that approach would work remains untested.

The full study was published in the journal Biological Psychiatry: Cognitive Neuroscience and Neuroimaging.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

It's one of the best gifts you can give to coffee lovers this holiday season.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

It really doesn't get fresher than this. A Storyville coffee subscription delivers delicious beans right to your doorstep.

We’ve all been there. Standing in the grocery store staring at dozens of coffee brands and trying to decide which one might perk us up, only to end up scowling at a subpar cup of sludge the next morning. Well, those days are over because Storyville, one of Seattle’s best coffee shops, now delivers freshly roasted beans straight to your door.

I tried a Storyville coffee subscription, and I assure you that you’ll never have to waste your time (or money) on stale beans again. Here’s everything you need to know about this caffeinated club.

Storyville is one of the highest-rated coffee shops in Seattle, with locations at Pike Place and at 1st and Madison. They also have a roasting studio you can tour to see exactly where your coffee beans are roasted before making their way to your door. But if you can’t make it to Seattle, they also offer coffee subscription services with nationwide shipping.

The brand sources premium-grade green coffees and roasts each bean varietal individually before blending. Beans are roasted on the darker end of medium and feature a rich, natural sweetness with notes of chocolate and caramel. Unlike many other dark roasts, this coffee has a smooth finish and no bitterness. Best of all, Storyville ships its coffee immediately after roasting.

Storyville subscriptions are highly customizable in order to fit your needs. First, choose between whole beans and single-serve pods. Then choose the quantity and delivery frequency that best suits your needs. You can pause, skip or cancel via text at any time. Moreover, you’ll receive 20% off, among other perks.

I am a coffee fiend. On any given day, I’m three cups in by 9 a.m. That’s why I couldn’t wait to get my hands on a bag of real Seattle coffee.

My order arrived on Wednesday, October 28. I received a bag of Prologue coffee beans, Sunrise espresso beans and Epilogue decaf coffee beans. I knew the roast dates would be stamped on the bags, but I was amazed to see that my beans had been roasted just days prior, on October 25 and October 27. Talk about fresh!

I opened the Prologue first and poured the entire bag into the bean hopper of my De’Longhi Rivelia. After brewing, I took a sip before adding my cream and sugar and was amazed by how smooth it was. It was fresh and flavorful without a hint of bitterness. I love a dark roast and could drink this all day long.

The next day, I opened the Sunrise espresso beans. I used it to make a regular cup of coffee followed by an Americano. Like the Prologue, the beans were clearly fresh, and the coffee was exceptionally smooth! I’ll be saving this bag along with the decaf for espresso martinis with my girlfriends.

As a dark roast gal, I’ve had my fair share of coffee that tastes bitter or burnt. I didn’t get any notes of bitterness from Storyville coffee. I’ll also point out that a bag of Prologue coffee lasted one week. Given how much coffee I drink plus the fact that my husband tends to make a cup here and there, I’m not surprised we went through it so quickly. Luckily, the subscription is completely customizable. If you drink as much coffee as I do, you’ll want to order two or three bags at a time. But if you just enjoy one cup per day, then one bag at a time will suffice.

That said, these beans are so fresh we wouldn’t be surprised if you start drinking more coffee than usual. And we certainly wouldn’t blame you!

It’s also easy to send a subscription as a gift. Simply head over to their gifting page and opt for three, five, eight or 12 prepaid shipments.

GM Patents Launch Control That Watches the Drag Strip Christmas Tree, Launches for You

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The recent patent describes a system that manages the brakes and throttle to deliver remarkably consistent drag strip blasts away from the line.

Getting a great launch at the drag strip takes more than horsepower and sticky tires. Drivers also need to time their start perfectly; even a tenth of a second can make a difference. Now, General Motors has patented technology that could take that last bit of human involvement out of the equation entirely.

The system uses the vehicle's cameras to watch the drag strip's Christmas Tree, predict when the light will turn green, and launch the car automatically. Better yet, it can actually begin preparing the launch before the green light illuminates. We might be about to see the age of push-button drag racing.

The technology is detailed in U.S. Patent 12,780,203, titled “Methods and Systems for Coordinating Launch Control for Vehicles for Racing.” Originally filed in January 2025, the application was published in July 2026 before finally receiving its patent grant on November 3.

At its core, the system builds on the launch control technology already available in modern performance cars. Right now, most launch control systems work very similarly: the driver applies full brakes and full throttle, and holds both pedals until it's time to go; they let off the brake, and the car rockets away. GM's proposed technology takes over most of that process.

The craziest bit is just how it manages all of this. First, it uses on-board cameras to identify the Christmas tree at the drag strip. Once it recognizes the lights, it notifies the driver and prompts them to activate launch control. Those who want to try this new method could control it much the same as they do with existing systems; the patent describes drivers putting their feet on both pedals and then, once the automatic launch system takes over, the driver can remove both feet and let the car work.

The car can maintain braking pressure and staging torque automatically, even after the driver releases the pedals. The clever part comes next: rather than simply waiting for the green light, the software identifies the sequence of yellow lights and compares it against stored timing patterns to calculate precisely when the race will begin.

That's key, because different events run the lights at different times. For example, a Pro Tree illuminates all three amber lights simultaneously before turning green 0.4 seconds later, whereas Sportsman Tree illuminates the amber lights sequentially with half-second intervals. GM's system could account for those differences automatically. The system is even capable of calculating the delay between commanding movement from the vehicle and the vehicle actually moving forward.

That all said, this is just a patent for now. GM doesn't mention any specific vehicle or plan for production in its filing. If it does bring it to market, we'd be eager to see how close it can come to cutting a perfect light.

I know that I'm not the only one who has always appreciated GOG as a sacred sanctuary for classic PC titles, but its latest event proves the platform is doing much more than just keeping old games running.

That's because, to celebrate its 18th anniversary, the team held its first-ever showcase, and it was packed with updates that have me genuinely excited about where the platform is heading – and you should be too!

Broadcast today, Thursday, November 5, on YouTube, the first-ever GOG Showcase caught everyone's attention with a variety of announcements, ranging from new games and features to an entirely new publishing initiative.

For starters, co-founder Miho Kinski perfectly summed up the platform's philosophy at the beginning of the event, making it clear that its mission remains unchanged: "We wanted games to live forever. Not for a season, not until the servers shut down, but for as long as people want them to play." And honestly, that message really resonated throughout the presentation.

Bit Kid shadowdrops Knytt Classic for free on Steam and GOG.

However, perhaps the biggest surprise was the reveal of Retrovibe by GOG, a new publishing initiative focused on bringing previously unreleased games with a "classic soul" to modern audiences. Fans can look forward to bringing lost games back from the dead.

Eighteen years of keeping games alive is no small feat, and based on these announcements, the next chapter for GOG looks just as bright. As the team emphasized during its first-ever showcase, "18 years of making games live forever together." I've always relied on the platform to revisit some of my favorite childhood titles, but with these new social features and its expansion into publishing, I have a feeling I'll be spending a lot more time exploring its library in the years to come!

Core Scientific to Wind Down Bitcoin Mining By Q4 2026 as AI Takes Over

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Core Scientific plans to fully exit Bitcoin mining operations by the end of Q4 2026.

AI data-center workloads can generate far more value from the same power capacity than Bitcoin mining.

Riot Platforms, MARA, HIVE and Hut 8 are also shifting toward AI and HPC infrastructure.

Core Scientific, one of the biggest names in Bitcoin mining, plans to wind down its mining operations by Q4 2026. It is not alone. Other major mining firms, including MARA Holdings, Riot Platforms, and HIVE Digital, have already begun shifting away from traditional Bitcoin mining.

But what is driving these major companies to shift their mining businesses? The reasons may surprise you.

During its Q3 2026 earnings call, Core Scientific confirmed that it expects to wind down its self-mining operations by 2026. From 2027, the company will focus on providing data center space for AI and high-performance computing.

The shift is already visible in its financial results.

Core Scientific shared its Q3 2026 results this morning. Key updates:– Data center revenue grew nearly 60% from last quarter to $215.4M, driven by continued capacity delivery to our customers.– As of September 30, 2026, we are billing for 485MW of capacity, generating over… pic.twitter.com/IIGp6p1tzm

Core Scientific generated $215.4 million in colocation revenue during Q3, up nearly 60% from the previous quarter. Colocation accounted for 93% of total revenue, while Bitcoin self-mining generated just $12.3 million. Adjusted EBITDA also jumped to $133.6 million, pushing the margin to 57%.

The company has already reduced its active mining fleet by nearly 50% from the end of 2025.

It even paid $41.9 million to end its Bitcoin mining equipment agreement with Block and Proto, ending future equipment deliveries.

The economics of Bitcoin mining have become increasingly difficult for large public miners.

Following the April 2024 halving, the block reward was cut from 6.25 BTC to 3.125 BTC. For public mining companies, the average cost of producing one Bitcoin has climbed to around $88.341. When Bitcoin trades at or below $80,000, miners can lose money on every coin they produce. Even when Bitcoin briefly rises above $80,000, profit margins remain very thin.

These changes have put pressure on mining profits. Core Scientific said weaker mining conditions contributed to a $266.5 million loss in value on its mining-related assets earlier this year.

Electricity costs are also pushing miners to consider other businesses. According to an energy infrastructure analyst, one megawatt of power can generate roughly $1.9 million over five years from Bitcoin mining, compared with about $7.9 million from AI data-center workloads.

This makes AI data centers a more profitable option for companies that already have large power facilities. Instead of using that electricity to mine Bitcoin, they can rent their facilities to AI companies through long-term contracts.

Core Scientific is already doing that. Its AMD and Neocloud agreement covers about 530 MW, while AMD also has reservation rights for another 1,925 MW of potential capacity.

Several other major miners are following a similar path. MARA Holdings sold about 15,133 BTC for roughly $1.1 billion in March and cut its workforce by 15% as it redirected capital toward balance-sheet strengthening and other opportunities.

Riot Platforms is taking the AI route even further. It signed a 20-year lease for 191 MW of critical IT capacity with a major AI lab, with an estimated $9.1 billion in contract revenue over the initial term.

HIVE is winding down its Bitcoin mining operations in Sweden while expanding its AI and HPC infrastructure in Canada.

The shift is becoming significant across the sector. At least 35 EH/s of computing power is scheduled to leave publicly listed Bitcoin miners as companies redirect capacity toward AI and high-performance computing.

Canva Is Cutting Employees After Its ‘Hardest Year Yet.’ Here’s What Their Unusually ‘Generous’ Severance Packages Include.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Forget benefits that help you stay. This package is designed to help you move on.

You have just lost your job. Can your employer help you find the next one?

That’s the question thousands of workers face at Canva, a graphic design platform launched in 2013. This week, Canva said that it is laying off, changing roles or relocating 1,271 employees, or about 22% of its workforce.

The move is a major restructuring that arrives in the wake of Canva’s “hardest year yet,” according to a memo sent to employees by co-founder and CEO Melanie Perkins. She mentioned that in the past year, the company’s growth slowed for the first time in 10 years.

“After exploring a wide range of alternatives, I’ve come to the difficult conclusion that small changes won’t be enough,” Perkins wrote. “To set ourselves up for the next decade, we need to re-found Canva, getting back to our roots of small, empowered teams.”

Perkins said that all affected employees will receive at least six months of pay regardless of where they live.

Typically, any equity Canva employees hold vests after a year with the company. However, laid-off workers will be eligible to keep their equity regardless of how long they’ve been with Canva.

Departing employees will also receive another 12 months of healthcare coverage in countries where Canva currently offers it, plus 12 months of access to its employee assistance program, Canva Care, for themselves and their families.

Canva’s approach reflects a broader shift toward more career support for departing employees, according to Greg Simpson, who leads the global career transition and mobility practice at talent advisory firm LHH.

“It does not surprise us to see an offer that’s like this,” Simpson said in a recent interview with Business Insider.

Canva is also offering individual coaching, interview preparation and a year of LinkedIn Premium to departing employees. The company is assigning a significant portion of its recruiting team to review resumes and practice interviews.

Employees can opt into an alumni talent directory that Canva will share with investors, partners and its wider industry network to help them find new roles. They can also keep their company MacBooks, because “we know not everyone has a personal laptop,” Perkins wrote in the memo.

The company is also putting money behind entrepreneurship. Through its new Canva Collective Fund, the company will offer $10,000 grants to departing employees who want to start a business or launch a community project. Anyone leaving as part of these changes has until December 14 to register interest. Canva will not take an ownership stake in anything they create.

Canva will keep Slack and Gmail open through the end of the week, giving affected employees time to absorb the news and say goodbye to colleagues on their own terms.

In an interview with Newsweek, KnotDating co-founder and CEO Jasveer Singh praised Canva’s handling of the layoffs, calling it “a master-class.”

He said the company’s announcement was “thoughtful, honest and generous.”

XRP Builds Bullish Case While Platform Flows Spike

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Home » Ripple (XRP) » XRP Builds Bullish Case While Platform Flows Spike

The OG altcoin just formed an Inverse Head-and-Shoulders as big-time investors cash out from exchanges.

XRP is drawing attention after a sharp rise in exchange outflows coincided with whale accumulation and a developing inverse head-and-shoulders pattern. The setup points to tighter supply, but a breakout still needs confirmation on the chart.

Around 01:00 UTC, roughly 13.24 million XRP left exchanges. That kind of withdrawal reduces the amount of tokens sitting ready to sell. It does not guarantee higher prices on its own, but it shrinks the immediate supply that can hit the market if buyers appear.

2/5 Today, XRP exchange outflows spiked. Around 01:00 UTC, roughly 13.24 million $XRP were withdrawn from exchanges, potentially reducing the supply immediately available to sell. pic.twitter.com/PG3Y33f1Yx

Price has been consolidating near $1.30 while the outflow hit.

The largest wallets increased their holdings by about 60 million XRP over the past week. It is unclear whether those tokens came directly from the exchange withdrawals, but both metrics move in the same direction.

Large holders adding size while tokens leave exchanges is the classic combination that often precedes tighter supply.

On the daily chart, XRP price appears to be forming the right shoulder of a potential inverse head-and-shoulders bottom.

4/5 Now look at the daily chart: $XRP appears to be forming the right shoulder of a potential inverse head-and-shoulders bottom.I’m watching for a move toward the neckline at $1.60. A break above that resistance would confirm the pattern and put $2.10 in focus. pic.twitter.com/9bcXItpUlj

The neckline sits near $1.60. A clean break above it would confirm the pattern and put a measured target near $2.10 in focus.

Price is currently trading around $1.30 to $1.32, still well below that level, so the pattern remains a developing setup rather than a completed breakout.

Exchange outflows and whale buying strengthen the case by reducing available supply and showing demand from large holders. Neither data point forces a breakout.

The chart still needs to clear $1.60 before the inverse head-and-shoulders is confirmed and the $2.10 target comes into play.

Until then, the flows stay in a supportive background while price decides whether the pattern completes.

Meta warns that election backlash against data centers could make AI expansion harder

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Meta president Dina Powell McCormick said that the Democratic wins in the midterm elections are going to make it a lot harder for companies to build data centers, adding that the industry has “not done as good a job as we needed to explain the benefits of these investments.”

Speaking at the WSJ Tech conference on Wednesday, the Meta exec pointed out that the backlash against data centers — which was very much on the ballot during this election season — was also tied to people’s feelings about AI technology overall.

“The visible sort of manifestation of AI is a data center, and it’s something that people can say, ‘I don’t want this in my community,’” she explained. “I think there’s an underlying bigger issue, which is how people feel about AI in general.”

Meta believes it could turn around the negative consumer sentiment around AI if it’s able to demonstrate the value, not only of the data centers coming to local communities, but also in the technology itself.

“The stakes of who builds the technology that cures cancer, that gives access to education, that is also a very fundamental difference … Mark [Zuckerberg, Meta CEO] is very ‘long human,’ and he’s betting on humans more than machines,” Powell McCormick explained. “And that’s what we mean when we say the future is for everyone. That people should not be left behind. But the way to ensure that is that this technology is democratized, that people have access to it in a very fair and transparent way,” she said.

The exec also touted Meta’s experience in building data centers, saying that the company has been doing so for 20 years, as it takes a lot of compute power to offer the products it does in the social networking space. Today, the company has data centers in 23 states, she said, and five of them, including one in Texas, are planned as projects of 1 gigawatt or more.

But Meta is now worried that it could lose some of its investments after politicians ran on anti-data-center platforms during the midterms.

“I think it’s going to be harder,” Powell McCormick said, describing what the future of building data centers looks like. “It has been hard, I should say, but I also think that it’s becoming clear what models work, and the states want those models that work.”

Specifically, communities only want data centers when it benefits them in some way, she explained, laying out the approach that Meta today uses to close its data center deals in local communities.

Successful projects see Meta paying for its own electricity, directing any tax surpluses as a result of the data centers toward teachers and first responders, keeping the labor force local, and getting bipartisan support for the project, among other things.

Going forward, she expects data center projects to come under more review and be weighed against whether the constituents actually want them.

Meta has pinned its hopes on its AI investments, particularly its consumer AI agent Muse scaling far beyond the 10 million users it has today, to become a business that one day serves billions. But even if that’s not the case, it believes that its investments in data centers will not be for nothing, as the compute itself will be valuable.

“We’re getting calls every day to lease that compute [we’ve built], and so one way or another, we’re seeing a path to monetization,” Powell McCormick said.
