Here’s What It Takes to Keep a Pool Running During the Winter, According to Experts

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Before you dive in, it’s important to understand the risks and expenses involved.

When most people think of backyard swimming pools, relaxing outdoors in the summertime immediately comes to mind. But some homeowners don’t want the swimming season to end when temperatures drop. Fans of cold-water swimming are starting to consider ways to enjoy their pools throughout the winter as a part of an at-home wellness experience.

“Ten years ago, most people didn’t think winter swims were possible, especially in colder climates,” says Karen Larson, cofounder of Soake Pools. “Times have changed because these days, they’re seeing neighbors, friends, and others do it and post the proof on social media.”

The increasing interest in cold-water experiences is part of a broader wellness trend. In American College of Sports Medicine's 2026 Worldwide Fitness Trends report, cold therapies, including cold plunges, cryotherapy, and cold showers, earned the 17th spot among fitness trends for the year, up from number 20 in its 2025 report.

If you’ve previously closed your swimming pool around Labor Day but are interested in making cold-water swimming a part of your daily wellness routine, there’s a lot to keep in mind. Not every existing pool is a good candidate for keeping open for winter swimming, and it’s important to understand the risks and expenses involved. But if you’re curious, here’s what to consider about keeping a pool open year-round.

Why is cold-water swimming becoming more common? There are numerous purported mental and wellness benefits, from athletic recovery to stress relief and better sleep. A 2025 systematic review and meta-analysis published in PLOS ONE found that cold-water immersion led to a reduction in stress for 12 hours afterward as well as improvements in sleep quality and overall quality of life.

“Winter swimming often creates a time where friends and family can come together without distractions,” adds Larson.

That said, while swimming during the colder months has its perks, there are also several risks to keep in mind. A 2020 review in the International Journal of Environmental Research and Public Health found that cold-water immersion can trigger an immediate shock response, leading to involuntary gasping, hyperventilation, and a rapid increase in heart rate and blood pressure. These reactions can make it difficult to control your breathing and increase your risk of drowning. Cold temps can also impair your muscle strength and nerve function, making it challenging to swim safely.

It’s a good idea to acclimate to the cold water gradually and avoid prolonged exposure. You’ll also want to make sure there’s someone else home while you take your cold-water plunge in the pool.

In addition, if you have underlying conditions like heart disease, consult your doctor before all else, as the cold water may place additional strain on your heart.

If you’re trying to consider whether your pool is a good candidate to keep open during the colder months, these are the things to look into.

The cost of keeping a swimming pool open for winter swimming will depend on your location, size, desired water temperature, and the type of heater you use.

“Routine pool maintenance generally remains about the same year-round, although chemical consumption may decrease depending on how frequently the pool is used,” explains Jim Eisch, Jr., vice president of operations at Pinch A Penny Pool Patio Spa. “The biggest expense to consider in the winter is heating the pool.”

For this reason, prospective winter swimmers may want to consider an upgrade to their heating system. Instead of using pricey natural gas heating or a traditional heat pump, Eisch says that inverter technology is one of the most affordable ways to maintain a swimmable pool temperature year-round.

For example, if you live in Florida and have a 10,000-gallon swimming pool, Eisch estimates your annual heating costs will be $2,467 with a natural gas heater, $741 with a traditional heat pump, and $434 for a heat/cool pump that uses inverter technology.

As aforementioned, the goal with wintertime swimming is not to be soaking in bathwater-like temperatures while it’s freezing outside. For safety purposes, winter swims are quick, and the heating system is to keep your chlorinated pool water well above the 19-degree Fahrenheit freezing point.

Unsurprisingly, a standard 14-by-28-foot swimming pool is going to be one of the hardest to use effectively year-round, unless you live in a region with a mild wintertime climate.

“You can technically use a large pool year-round, but it’s difficult to cover all its surface area well enough to hold in the heat, so heating bills can climb fast,” says Larson.

Therefore, smaller sizes like a 10-by-16-foot cocktail pool or plunge pool are better candidates for keeping open. Dedicated winter swimmers should also invest in an easy on-and-off cover—or a motorized auto cover—for added protection.

Regular pool cleaning and maintenance are essential, and this upkeep over the winter is no exception.

“Check the filtration system regularly to make sure it’s circulating smoothly, monitor the water chemistry, and plan for power outages—because there’s a good chance they will happen,” Larson explains. For this reason, you should also invest in a generator that can keep the pump running if the power goes out.

Safe pool access is just as important as any other aspect of the pool’s design. That’s why a nonslip surface, excellent drainage, and ample lighting are key.

“Your design should also provide a clear path from the house to the water,” says Neha Gupta, interior designer and founder at NU Curations.

Especially in the wintertime when ice and snow are a factor in your region, make sure you have a safe and smooth exit plan for getting out of the water, bundled up, and into your warm home without any obstacles. And then, of course, remember to cover the pool back up to keep it protected and ready for your next plunge.

OpenAI released a new cybersecurity model. Is that enough?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

Max Justicz, the chief technology officer and co-founder of the startup Charge Robotics, used an OpenAI model to find a severe vulnerability that — if exploited — could allow hackers to take control of servers running Ubuntu, a widely used open source Linux-based operating system.

Justicz, who has a hobbyist’s interest in cybersecurity and previously found a vulnerability in the same codebase, told TechCrunch that he found this issue using GPT 5.6 Sol, released in July, through the AI maker’s Daybreak Blue, a program that gives cybersecurity defenders access to powerful frontier models.

“I literally just aimed ChatGPT at the same body of code and said ‘I found this bug previously that was fixed several years ago, please find bugs as bad or worse,’ and it came back in two hours with this,” Justicz said. “It’s extremely convoluted and hard for a human to find, but it works!”

He reported the issue to the Debian Project, which maintains the software in question and issued a patch to fix the vulnerability. There has been no known evidence of exploitation. Still, the ease with which he found it suggests that we’ve only seen the tip of the iceberg when it comes to potential exploits that even novice hackers can utilize with the help of AI models.

“I can only imagine what one could do with more time and access to less neutered models. We should expect some extremely crazy bugs to be found and exploited over the next few months,” Justicz wrote in a social media thread discussing his finding. “This might be obvious to most folks here, but there is currently huge alpha in taking some code or binary someone has not audited yet and just aiming a powerful AI model at it.”

OpenAI appears to have noticed. Today, the AI giant announced a new model focused on cybersecurity, ChatGPT Cyber, would be made available to the highest tier of users in the cybersecurity program, Daybreak Red, which allows offensive cybersecurity researchers tools for vulnerability hunting and development. Yesterday, OpenAI said that the same model had been used to scan and prepare patches for vulnerabilities in the Linux kernel, finding 948 potential issues. OpenAI also found 81 “novel” vulnerabilities in Firefox, the web browser.

“Our goal is to dramatically expand Daybreak, bringing advanced security research to organizations of every size, independent researchers, and open-source maintainers,” the frontier lab said in a statement.

OpenAI has insisted that there is a narrow window of time for the people who safeguard digital infrastructure to take action before AI models become even better at hacking and easier to access. For cyber defenders, however, it can feel like the frontier labs have unleashed a mountain of new work that outstrips their resources. Justicz said that he personally spent about $5,000 on subscriptions and tokens to find several exploits.

Besides Daybreak’s model access, OpenAI has also announced a $1 billion initiative to support cybersecurity professionals in the program who work on critical infrastructure like the electrical grid or water systems.

The Daybreak program has its own critiques. Justicz notes that the frontier lab appears to have recently “nerfed” Daybreak Blue to make it less useful at finding vulnerabilities. On one hand, it may be good to limit access from potential bad actors — Justicz said he just uploaded a photo of his driver’s license to join the program. On the other, cybersecurity professionals say they have one hand tied if they can’t use frontier technology while bad actors can deploy open-weight models with abliterated guardrails.

Still, OpenAI says just “thousands” of cybersecurity professionals are participating in the Daybreak program, versus a far larger number of potential hackers.

It’s unclear if frontier labs are devoting enough of their resources to this issue. Eric Wallace, a researcher on OpenAI’s technical staff, said that “we are now allocating a significant amount of our frontier compute towards discovering and patching vulnerabilities in open source codebases, fully pro bono.”

But the Linux bug hunt cost $1.2 million, which is significantly less than the approximate $6 million token spend the company invested in finding a potential solution to a famous problem of fluid dynamics.

If there is a narrow window of time to patch the digital infrastructure the world depends on, more urgency may be required.

Election Night 2026 Ratings: NBC News is the Most Watched Network

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Steve Kornacki‘s data wizardry helped propel NBC News to the No. 1 spot in broadcast and cable in total viewers and the Adults 25-54 demo during Tuesday night’s coverage of the 2026 midterm elections.

NBC News’ performance comes as, according to Nielsen Media Research, a total of 31.2 million viewers across 13 networks tuned in between 8 and 11 p.m. ET to watch the blue wave sweep across the nation as the Democratic Party won both chambers of Congress. (All times Eastern) This year’s numbers were significantly better than the last midterm election, up +23% compared to 2022.

Hover or tap a point for the exact viewers, the programming window, and the number of networks.

During primetime, NBC News registered 6.74 million total viewers and 2.794 million demo viewers. Compared to 2022, NBC News was up by triple digits, 117% in total viewers and 164% in the demo.

In second place in both measured categories was ABC News, with 5.19 million total viewers and 1.672 million demo viewers. This represented gains of 57% in total viewers and 86% in the demo compared to the last midterm election cycle.

On the cable news front, Fox News led the way in total viewers, while CNN was the top network in the prized demo. Fox News had 4.315 million total viewers and 771,000 demo viewers. Compared to 2022, Fox News was down 42% in total viewers and 59% in the demo.

Meanwhile, CNN finished second among the cable networks with 4.147 million total viewers, but was No. 1 in the demo with 1.512 million viewers. Compared to 2022, CNN saw gains of 59% in total viewers and 43% in the demo.

Are Young Men the New Kings of the Swing Vote?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Two years ago, it seemed that the young men of Gen Z were shaping up to be hardcore conservatives. More than half of them voted for Donald Trump’s second presidency, refuting the idea that young people tend to lean progressive. Liberal media panicked about finding “a Joe Rogan of the left” who could reach them, and conservatives took a victory lap. “Gen Z Has Turned Right!” Fox News’s Greg Gutfeld announced, citing a report from the Harvard Kennedy School that highlighted, among other insights, the ways social media has led to an increase in conservatism among young folks. A Turning Point USA Poll from around the same time declared Generation Z “the most conservative generation in decades.”

On Tuesday night, though, as a blue tidal wave flipped states from Iowa to Texas, it was young men who comprised a critical segment of the new Democratic coalition. As reported by CIRCLE, a non-partisan research organization at Tufts University, white men between the ages of 18 to 29 voted blue by a 10-point margin; in 2024, exit polling indicated that 56% of all young men voted for Donald Trump. Even more remarkably, young people across the board voted for Democratic congressional candidates by a 40% margin, according to data from NBC.

“In the wake of 2024, there was this whole catastrophizing panic,” says Aidan Kohn-Murphy, the founder of Gen Z for Change (formerly known as TikTok for Biden) and a prominent political influencer. “Oh my God, everything we thought about Gen Z is wrong. It's actually a conservative generation. And no, that's not what was happening. I mean, a huge part of Gen Z just didn't show up to vote because they didn't feel like they had a reason.”

For Democratic strategist Eric Stern, connecting with men on an emotional level was critical in getting them out to vote. Stern has worked on Ruben Gallego and John Fetterman’s campaigns; his political ad agency, FIGHT, has produced spots for Mayor Zohran Mamdani, Senator-Elect Abdul El-Sayed, and Governor-Elect Gina Hinojosa, to name a few. This election cycle, Stern sought to change voters’ perception of what a Democrat can look like.

“Our most notable ad of the general election was Bob [Brooks] standing in front of a literal dumpster fire and then putting it out because he's a firefighter,” he said. Brooks, now the Representative-elect in the Lehigh Valley, flipped his seat on Tuesday. “A ton of Latino men are really into boxing and MMA fighting, and so Ruben [Gallego] held events around boxing,” Stern told me. For Texas’ Hinojosa, who'll become the state’s first Democratic governor in 35 years when she's sworn in in January, that meant running an ad during Mexican boxing champion Canelo Álvarez's match against Christian Mbilli, an event with a massive Latino viewing audience.

Still, Kohn-Murphy and Stern both argued that the main issue on the ballot this cycle was affordability. “If you look at polling, far and away the top issue for voters is cost of living,” Stern said. “And so you can run a million ads saying that ‘Jane Doe is for they/them,’ but it's not going to resonate with people in the same way if they can't afford to keep their lights on.”

When I asked Republican political consultant Kirsten Kukowski about young men voting blue, she brought up affordability too. After all, nearly nine months into the Iran War, a barrel of oil still costs $100. “Men and young men voted with their bank accounts,” she offered. “And for the last several years, that's how they've been voting. And I think that's how Donald Trump won that group two years ago, because he was talking about affordability and inflation.”

Kukowski, the former Communications Director for the Republican National Committee, now runs her own firm, K2 and Company, out of Minneapolis. “At some point here, people expect results,” she continued. “People expected results from Donald Trump, and they didn't see that. So now we're on the clock again for another two years.”

Met Gala 2027: Costume Institute Announces New Theme After John Galliano Controversy

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The 2027 Met Gala has a new focus. On November 5, Vogue, alongside the Metropolitan Museum of Art Costume Institute, announced its new spring 2027 exhibition and Met Gala theme. And its new focus is on the “extraORDINARY: Fashion and the Poetics of the Everyday,” which will be curated by Andrew Bolton alongside Stephanie Kramer and Emily Mushaben. This pivot comes after designer John Galliano pulled out of the 2027 Met Gala months after political and civic leaders raised concerns over his previous racist and antisemitic comments that led to his termination as Dior’s creative director in 2011.

The exhibition will be “devoted to the undersung beauty and allure of quotidian garments—think the aforementioned t-shirts and jeans, plus raincoats, sweatshirts, puffer jackets, and more. It will ask visitors ‘to see the everyday anew, and come at its subject from two perspectives: First, exploring how designers have reinvented and elevated archetypal garments to runway-worthy creations and then, secondly, highlight how humble, even ‘disposable’ materials—like paper, aluminum, and plastic—are transformed into wearable art.”

“It was really important to come up with an exhibition that wasn’t just a masterwork show or something that we could do a little bit more easily,” Bolton also said. “That was really important to me: to come up with an idea that wasn’t a substitute. And it had to have some sort of contemporary resonance.”

Before this switch, the museum stated that the original exhibit, John Galliano: Horizons, was meant to “consider how artistic achievement should be understood alongside ethical accountability.” So now, this exploration of the extraORDINARY seems to be more focused on design and creation from a more sustainable viewpoint.

So now that fashion’s biggest night is back on track with a new theme in hand, it will be interesting to see how all our favorite celebrities interpret this new theme. The Met Gala 2027 will take place on May 3, and the exhibit will open to the public on May 9, 2027, and run through January 9, 2028.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. She is a 2024 Forbes 30 Under 30: Media honoree, and her debut romance novella PASSING GAME is set to release March of 2027 (831 Stories/Simon & Schuster).

Ask a Local: Where to Go in Nairobi According to Runner Emily Chepkor

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

"Nairobi is a city best understood in motion," says Emily Chepkor, founder of We Run Nairobi, the city's biggest and most popular run club. She's from a small town in Kenya, Kapsabet, that has produced more world-class runners per square mile than anywhere else on earth. While the country is known worldwide for its running heritage, Nairobi hadn't quite caught up to the hype until Chepkor came along.

She runs as a hobby, and is a 12-time marathoner (and two-time Boston qualifier) who felt that, though Kenya's running reputation was unmatched internationally, the runner lifestyle itself wasn't accessible to everyday Kenyans—elite athletes only. She set out to change that and bring running closer to all Nairobians, starting with a weekly run club that began with just her and three friends. It slowly grew through word of mouth and especially TikTok, into something bigger, built on a simple idea: no registration, no fees, you just show up and run alongside Emily.

That run has become a defining weekly ritual for Nairobians, drawing over 1,200 people every Saturday morning. It's now etched into the city's identity. The same way New York is known for its bagels, Nairobi is known for the We Run Nairobi neighborhood runs, a cultural landmark for locals and tourists alike.

Below, Emily tells us about some of her favorite things to do in the city she calls home.

Karura Forest is “without a doubt” the best place to run in Nairobi, if you ask Emily.

"Karura Forest, without a doubt, is the most scenic route in the city”, she says. “I run the Sigiria side, through Gate F; it's quieter, more canopied. My dog Biscuit and I do the 10-kilometer loop most Sundays, crossing little rivers every kilometer. If you want something gentler, the 5-kilometer route is just as lovely." Her Saturday club runs the same way: "We're completely pace-agnostic. First-timers run next to marathoners".

Karura is the second largest urban forest in the world after the Tijuca Forest in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil, and provides the perfect place for an outdoor run.

“Kengele's is always a safe bet whatever's on, whether it's World Cup, rugby, athletics, you'll find a good crowd," says Chepkor. "What I love is how the embassies get involved: around big sporting events, they'll partner with venues across the city to host public watch parties. I'll never forget the British High Commission's watch party in Karura Forest, the day Sebastian Sawe broke the two-hour marathon barrier. The whole country was locked in, and experiencing that moment surrounded by hundreds of people felt incredibly special.”

The McMillan Memorial Library is one of her favorite places for some peace and quiet, and some history.

“The McMillan Memorial Library is a perfect little time capsule of Nairobi, architecturally and literally—it's currently being renovated by Book Bunk, founded by two Kenyan women committed to restoring our public libraries," she says. "I also love the Nairobi Gallery, home to Point Zero, the spot from which all distances in Kenya are measured.”

“Tigoni is a small town only 30 to 40 minutes from Nairobi, but it feels much farther away. The city gives way to rolling tea plantations and narrow country roads. I start at Brackenhurst with coffee from Muna Tree Cafe on the terrace, then rent a bicycle and ride through the plantations, stopping wherever the view is too good to pass.” Back at Brackenhurst, she'll spread a blanket on the lawn to read, maybe doze off, then finish with tennis or a walk through the Indigenous forest. "It's the itinerary I'm proudest to take an out-of-towner on," Chepkor says.

"Sigiria Coffee House is my default—small, intimate, and full of dogs on Sunday mornings. On tennis mornings it's Waitabit, same easy vibe." For souvenirs, she sends visitors to two spots near each other: "Spinners Web has beautiful rugs, a café, and a used-books library. Just up the road, Tribal Gallery has grander pieces—restored Swahili doors, oversized mirrors—alongside smaller suitcase-friendly finds like ceramics and jewelry."

"Fig & Olive in Tigoni is part bodega, part restaurant, with a lawn where no one blinks if you swap your chair for the grass. In the city, it's the Tin Roof Café in Karen—moving from table to grass and back, especially post-run, and the salad bar is iconic. For something dressier that still softens outdoors, Matteo's: white tablecloths, al fresco seating, and little intimate corners I'm still discovering."

The Talisman Restaurant is a Nairobi stalwart.

Scoring a place by the fireplace is a coup.

"The Talisman Restaurant is a Nairobi stalwart. Scoring a corner table by the fireplace on a chilly evening is a date-night dream. For something more stylish, Inti has skyline views in every direction—some Friday nights it turns into an intimate DJ set." For the rest of the evening, she splits the city in two: “If you're after energy, Geco, alive with Nairobi's music scene, especially on Wednesdays. For actual conversation, Cave-à-Manger has an expansive wine list and keeps the music in the background.”

"Toi Market, with one caveat: Nairobi isn't really a vintage city in the curated, boutique sense— we're much more of an open-air, flea market vibe, with thousands of stalls. Toi is sprawling, busy, and a little chaotic. You'll need patience and a willingness to dig, but if you put in the time, you'll almost always come away with something great for a fraction of what you'd pay elsewhere."

“Nai Nami is properly off the beaten track; it's a walking tour of Nairobi's CBD led by former street children. You see downtown Nairobi through the eyes of people who know it intimately, and leave with a completely different understanding of the city. The Alliance Française is an unexpected answer, but it's one of Nairobi's great cultural hubs—plays, concerts, exhibitions, most of which are free, and the original stomping ground of Sauti Sol, our biggest boy band.”

A view of the city from Nairobi National Park

"Nairobi is the only city in the world with a national park so if you’ve only got one day, make it a wildlife day. I’d go for a game drive through Nairobi National Park at sunrise, then the elephant feeding at Sheldrick Wildlife Trust, and the Giraffe Centre to finish. Afterwards, a slow lunch nearby at either Cultiva, Talisman, or Matteo's. "

"I'd highly recommend coming to a We Run Nairobi Saturday. One of my favorite traditions is that we 'brand' every first-timer with a little sticker, so everyone knows it's your first Saturday. I'll encourage people to say hello, ask where you're visiting from, and recommend a coffee spot. You're moving together with actual Nairobians, in neighborhoods where we actually live. You're not being shown the city, you get to experience it alongside the people who call it home.”

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Binance, Deribit revoke SignalPlus API keys after fake Teams invite attack

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

SignalPlus said attackers compromised an engineer’s computer in a breach that resulted in losses of less than $82,000, which the crypto trading platform pledged to reimburse in full.

Exchanges Binance and Deribit said Thursday they had revoked API keys connected to crypto derivatives trading platform SignalPlus as a precaution following a security breach. Both exchanges said their own systems were unaffected, while Deribit warned that trading, monitoring and automation through SignalPlus had stopped working for its customers.

Binance co-founder Yi He also urged third-party platforms integrated with the exchange to review their security controls and monitor for suspicious activity, warning that the threat extended beyond any single company.

SignalPlus provides trading infrastructure for crypto derivatives, particularly options, allowing professional and institutional traders to manage positions across multiple exchanges through a single platform.

According to the company on X, the attack originated from a compromised Telegram account belonging to a contact at a third-party technology vendor. The attacker impersonated the contact and sent a fake Microsoft Teams meeting invitation to a SignalPlus engineer, compromising the employee’s computer.

The attacker remained dormant on the compromised computer before using the engineer’s access privileges to target SignalPlus’s internal network early Wednesday, the company said.

SignalPlus said it detected and contained the intrusion, with total losses of less than $82,000, and had begun fully reimbursing affected users. The company temporarily suspended its services and expects to restore operations over the next few days after completing security reviews and applying patches.

SignalPlus also acknowledged that it had not required IP whitelisting for exchange API access. “We take responsibility for this oversight,” the company said, adding that it would tighten API access requirements and strengthen internal security controls. IP whitelisting is a security measure that restricts API access to a predefined list of approved internet addresses.

Related: BitMart founder says security team ‘severely understaffed’ after new hack

The SignalPlus incident comes days after Binance disclosed details of a $6.3 billion hack on Oct. 29, which also involved compromised employee computers.

According to Binance’s postmortem, attackers posing as recruiters tricked an engineer into downloading a malicious file disguised as a coding assignment. The attackers subsequently used the compromised computer to gain access to additional systems and eventually breach the exchange’s wallet infrastructure.

Binance co-founder Yi He said Tuesday that the exchange had detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats in 2026, including fraudulent job interviews, malicious software packages and attempts to steal employee credentials.

Blackburn Rovers vs West Ham Preview & Lineups – Five Changes For Parker?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Blackburn Rovers v West Ham United EFL Championship Ewood Park Saturday 7 November 2026, 3pm GMT

Scott Parker had less than 24 hours to prepare West Ham United for Wednesday night’s extraordinary 3-3 draw at Cardiff City, but the second-half transformation should heavily influence the team he selects against Blackburn Rovers.

The Hammers were unrecognisable after the interval, recovering from three goals down through Divine Mukasa, Arne Engels and Max Kilman. Parker, Mark Noble and Jarrod Bowen clearly delivered a rousing half-time message, while the tactical and personnel changes completely altered the match.

West Ham remain eighth and four points behind second-placed Wolverhampton Wanderers, making victory at Ewood Park extremely important. Parker will have the international break to properly implement his methods, but first he must use the lessons from Cardiff to select a side capable of taking three points into it.

I have already covered the Cardiff match extensively, so there is little value in going over every detail again. What matters for this preview is the enormous contrast between West Ham’s two performances.

I very much detach the three goals conceded in the first half from what happened after the break. The opening 45 minutes looked like a continuation of everything we had witnessed under Nuno Espírito Santo, while the second offered the first glimpse of Parker’s influence.

Parker introduced Ezra Mayers, Mohamadou Kanté and Joël Piroe at half-time before later replacing Manor Solomon with Edvin Austbø. Those decisions gave West Ham greater energy, aggression and bite.. in short, the changes worked an absolute treat.

The substitutions worked, but so did the tactical changes. West Ham pressed higher, attacked space behind Cardiff’s defence and played with an urgency which had been completely absent before the break. As I discussed in my reaction to Parker’s first post-match interview, the new manager was particularly enthusiastic about the more aggressive, front-footed approach.

That should influence Parker’s selection at Blackburn far more than anything he can achieve during two brief training sessions.

Engels & Piroe can both expect to start against Blackburn

I think it is time to give Mads Hermansen a rest and select Alphonse Areola in goal. Hermansen has endured a difficult period, conceding nine times across the past three matches, and temporarily removing him from the firing line may benefit everybody.

Parker already knows Areola extremely well from their season together at Fulham. The goalkeeper was brought to Craven Cottage during Parker’s time as manager and performed impressively despite their eventual relegation.

That existing relationship could make this an obvious early change. Parker knows Areola’s strengths and temperament, while the goalkeeper will understand what the new head coach expects from him.

Replacing Kyle Walker-Peters is straightforward if he remains unavailable. Joël Veltman was sensational during the second half at Cardiff and should continue at right-back.

Veltman provided leadership, composure and attacking support for Bowen. At a time when West Ham’s defence has looked incredibly nervous, his experience is particularly valuable.

Mavropanos returned from his knee problem on Wednesday and should remain alongside Kilman. The partnership still needs work, but Parker has very little time to experiment before the international break.

The significant change should come at left-back. Ezra Mayers impressed again after replacing Morato at half-time and deserves an opportunity from the beginning.

Mayers offered more natural movement down the flank and was willing to attack the penalty area. He almost completed the comeback with a late volley and looked considerably more comfortable in the position than a centre-back being asked to operate as a full-back.

I do not see how Tomáš Souček can force his way into the starting XI. He nearly scored the winner at Cardiff with a late header, but both Engels and Mukasa deserve to retain their places.

Engels improved enormously after half-time and took his goal like a striker, timing his run perfectly before producing a confident finish. Mukasa also deserves to continue in his preferred attacking-midfield role after scoring West Ham’s first and helping change the momentum of the game.

Kanté has absolutely forced himself into the team. His energy, ball-carrying and aggression transformed the midfield, giving West Ham the ability to press Cardiff and move possession forward quickly.

The most sensible balance is therefore Engels and Kanté as the two deeper midfielders, with Mukasa ahead of them. Souček remains an excellent option from the bench, particularly when West Ham need a goal or greater aerial presence late in a game.

Joël Piroe must start in attack. His clever flick created Engels’ goal, but his overall movement and willingness to drop deeper also helped connect West Ham’s midfield with the forward line.

Piroe is the only recognised striker in the squad with a proven Championship goalscoring record, yet he has not received a consistent run of starts. Parker should now give him the opportunity to establish himself.

Austbø also deserves to replace Solomon on the left. The Norwegian was direct and positive after coming on, forcing a late save and immediately looking more dangerous than the player he replaced.

Bowen will naturally continue on the right, although Parker should allow him to drift into central areas. The captain ran the second half at Cardiff, creating all three West Ham goals and dragging the team back into a match which had appeared completely lost.

I expect Parker to retain the 4-2-3-1 formation but reward the players who changed the game at Cardiff. Areola, Mayers, Kanté, Austbø and Piroe should all come into the starting XI.

Blackburn have lost consecutive matches against Swansea City and Stoke City, but their victory over current leaders Middlesbrough demonstrates that West Ham cannot take them lightly.

Tony Mowbray’s team have won five Championship matches this season and will be determined to enter the international break with a response of their own. Ewood Park has not been a regular destination for the Hammers, with this our first league visit since the 2010/11 Premier League season.

Nevertheless, West Ham should travel north with genuine confidence following the Cardiff comeback. Parker now has evidence of which players responded to his instructions and which combinations gave the team greater balance.

This is his final match before receiving two uninterrupted weeks on the training pitch. A victory would reduce the gap to the automatic-promotion positions and give him valuable momentum before beginning the more substantial work of reshaping the team.

Blackburn’s recent defeats suggest this is an excellent opportunity for Parker to claim his first victory as West Ham head coach. The Hammers must still defend considerably better, but the second-half attacking performance at Cardiff showed what this squad can produce.

If Parker selects the players who drove that comeback and retains the more aggressive approach, I believe West Ham will have too much quality for Blackburn. There may still be another defensive mistake in this team, but I expect the Hammers to win convincingly enough.

Prediction: Blackburn Rovers 1-3 West Ham United

Kris Gonzo (commonly known to the West Ham community as Gonzo) is the co-founder of Hammers Chat (established 2014) and the lead video content creator and columnist for Claret and Hugh.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live. He previously served as an official video and content creator for West Ham United FC during the club's tenure at Upton Park and featured in the official documentary commemorating the historic final game at the Boleyn Ground.

A lifelong Hammer since the legendary 10-0 victory over Bury, when he isn't providing daily match analysis on the Hammers Chat YouTube Channel or broadcasting on X (@GONZObignose), he can be found walking his dog and restoring classic pinball machines.

The World of Hyatt personal and business credit cards can help you save money and enjoy valuable perks on your next Hyatt stay.

Right now, the personal card is offering its most lucrative bonus since February, making now a good time to apply.

The World of Hyatt program is known for offering some of the best redemption rates in the industry, along with a diverse portfolio of distinctive hotel brands.

Let's dive into these offers and help you decide whether to apply.

The World of Hyatt Credit Card (see rates and fees) is currently offering new cardholders four free nights (at Category 1-4 Hyatt hotels) after spending $5,000 on purchases in the first three months from account opening.

Per Hyatt's award chart, Category 1 Hyatt hotels can cost as few as 3,000 points per night, while Category 4 properties can cost as many as 25,000 points per night.

That means the value of this welcome offer depends on how you redeem the free nights. If you use all four certificates at Category 4 properties that would otherwise cost 25,000 points per night, you'd "use" 100,000 points. Based on TPG's November 2026 valuations, that puts the bonus's potential value at up to $1,650.

While this isn't the strongest welcome offer we've seen in the card's history, it does meet our threshold for when to apply.

IEEE Program Schools Preuniversity Educators On Tech Topics

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The TryEngineering workshops help teachers deliver engaging STEM lessons

An educator constructs a microchip model during the IEEE Making a Microchip lesson plan demonstration.

Now in their third year, the IEEE TryEngineering educator sessions continue to empower preuniversity teachers with specialized professional development programs on engineering topics and classroom-tested lessons.

This year’s sessions, held across the United States, have focused on educating teachers about artificial intelligence and semiconductor technology. One session was held in Egypt.

In all, 54 educators learned about the engineering design process this year. They received an overview of TryEngineering resources including the Making of a Microchip and Train Your Tree lesson plans.

Workshops are scheduled this year in Greece and Guatemala.

The TryEngineering program is administered by IEEE Educational Activities.

These teachers and guest speakers attended the two-day session held at the Florida Semiconductor Institute and SkyWater Technology chip manufacturing facility.IEEE Educational Activities

Central to the educator session program’s success is its university network. Host campuses welcome teachers into their facilities, where faculty provide background information about some of the technologies being taught, as well as possible engineering career paths and job opportunities for students.

This year’s sessions involved the educators in hands-on projects. Facilitators at each location were selected based on their experience in engineering education, and they worked with the TryEngineering staff on their presentations.

Sixteen teachers from nine schools participated in the session, held in Cairo. It was facilitated by Marwa Soudi, an IEEE member, and Senior Member Stamatis Dragoumanos. Both are past chairs of the IEEE preuniversity coordinating committee. GEMS provided outstanding STEM facilities for teachers, Saudi and Dragoumanos said. Participants received an overview of the Working With Wind Energy and Tall Tower Challenge lesson plans.

Another educator session is scheduled for later this year.

Educators from across the state gathered at the university’s Florida Semiconductor Institute, headquartered in Gainesville, and FSI’s NeoCity high-tech manufacturing campus near Orlando. The session was hosted by Roseanne Perez, an FSI workforce development program specialist, and Sophia Watson, FSI’s assistant director of workforce development. The two-day experience featured an inside look at microelectronics through a guided tour of a SkyWater Technology chip manufacturing facility.

Engineering instructor Mary Acken facilitated hands-on lesson demonstrations for the 15 attending teachers.

Reflecting on the workshop’s impact, one participant said the SkyWater tour was excellent, the instructors were great, and the teachers enjoyed learning from like-minded educators.

Michael Kozicki, an IEEE member, and Stefan Myhajlenko from the university’s engineering school welcomed 11 Arizona teachers to the Tempe campus.

The session was facilitated by Janet Hartkopf, an Arizona school leader and a former TryEngineering educator session participant. Hartkopf highlighted collaborative learning, the need for diverse perspectives in engineering education, and career paths.

The teachers received instruction on how to implement the hands-on lessons in their classrooms, and they toured the university’s NanoFab facility to observe microelectronics research.

“This training was amazing!” said a participant who leads an elementary after-school STEM club. “I loved the hands-on lessons and e-books, which I will definitely be using with my students. I am excited to explore all the resources on the TryEngineering website. The experts from ASU were great.”

Sixteen STEM educators attended this session, held at the university’s International Institute for Biosensing in Minneapolis. Its director, Patricia Khashayar, an IEEE member, introduced participants to the capabilities of the university’s Supercomputing Institute and Nano Center.

Under the guidance of Claire Dietz, an education and outreach coordinator, and Pam Schumacher, an instructor at St. Paul College, the teachers completed classroom-ready lessons including a photonics experiment using the Nano Center’s equipment. Schumacher attended last year’s TryEngineering educator session.

Nano Center Director Sarah Swisher, an IEEE senior member, provided an overview of semiconductor operations and highlighted growing job opportunities for the state’s young people.

The experience inspired all 16 teachers to plan student field trips to the Nano Center during the upcoming academic year, they said.

“Great class!” one participant said. “I’m excited to bring some of the TryEngineering materials back to our school.”

This year’s sessions built on the momentum from last year when, with financial support from Onsemi’s Giving Now program, nearly 80 teachers attended sessions across five U.S. states and Mexico.

Driven by that success and the demand for more, the IEEE Educational Activities Board continued its support. Upcoming global sessions are expected to expand the program’s international footprint.

“I appreciated the time to collaborate with peers on how to implement hands-on lessons,” one participating teacher this year said. Some participants said they planned follow-up visits with the experts and campus tours.

You can learn more about the sessions by emailing TryEngineering.

Debra Gulick is director of IEEE student and academic educational programs.

Earth has captured a new mini moon  — but it won't stick around for long

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Sign up to our monthly entertainment newsletter to keep up with all our coverage of the latest sci-fi and space movies, tv shows, games and books.

Discover this week's must-see night sky events, moon phases, and stunning astrophotos. Sign up for our skywatching newsletter and explore the universe with us!

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Earth has caught itself a new mini-moon, and it is sticking around for Christmas.

The space rock, designated 2026 QQ8, was first observed in August 2026 after entering orbit around Earth in April 2026. The discovery was reported by the International Astronomical Union's Minor Planet Center on Thursday (Oct. 29).

The stay of QQ8 is a temporary one. The mini-moon will remain in the thrall of Earth's gravity through December before breaking away in 2027.

Scientists have recreated the orbital path of QQ8, finding that it takes 359 days, or about 0.98 of an Earth year, to orbit the sun. QQ8 closely approaches Earth around once every ten years, but this time the object came so close that Earth has been able to gravitationally seize it.

According to the Minor Planet Center, the closest QQ8 will come to our planet during this stay is around 353,000 miles (568,000 kilometers). That is around 110,000 miles (177,000 km) further out than the average distance between Earth and the moon. Even if QQ8 were to come close enough to collide with Earth, it would likely be completely destroyed in our planet's atmosphere before it reaches the surface, as it is only around 6 feet (2 meters) in diameter.

This is far from the first time Earth has snagged itself a mini-moon, more accurately called a temporary satellite. In fact, research last year suggested that our planet could be orbited by as many as six mini-moons at any given time, with many of these being fragments of our more permanent natural satellite, the moon.

While QQ8 is tiny, it is larger than the average mini-moon. That is according to modelling earlier this year that suggested the average size for a temporary satellite is 75 centimeters (30 inches) across.

The same modelling suggested the average stay for such an object would be around nine months.

So if QQ8 was captured in April 2026, that means it should move away in January 2027.

The researchers behind those findings suggested that, were Earth to gravitationally grip a space rock around 10 feet (3 meters) wide, it could serve as a mini-moon for 10 to 20 years.

Robert Lea is a science journalist in the U.K. whose articles have been published in Physics World, New Scientist, Astronomy Magazine, All About Space, Newsweek and ZME Science. He also writes about science communication for Elsevier and the European Journal of Physics. Rob holds a bachelor of science degree in physics and astronomy from the U.K.’s Open University. Follow him on Twitter @sciencef1rst.

Buyers Stopped Believing Pitches. Give Them Something They Can Verify.

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Software now writes the pitch, sends it and even personalizes it, so buyers have learned to discount all of it. Here’s what works now.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Anyone can write a good pitch now. A decent cold email, a tidy case study, a landing page full of confident claims. It takes a tool and about 10 minutes. Software now sends the outreach too, in volumes no person could match. Even the personal touches are written by it.

Buyers know all of this, and they have adjusted. The businesses winning new customers right now stopped asking to be believed and started letting people check.

Think about what a pitch actually is. It is the seller describing their own work in the best possible light. That was always a little suspect. Now it is also free to produce at scale, which means every competitor sounds equally polished and equally sure of themselves.

Buyers have noticed. The TrustRadius 2026 B2B Buying Disconnect report surveyed 1,862 technology buyers in January 2026. Among the buyers who used AI during their purchase, 94% fact-checked its answers at least some of the time. They are not only doubting vendors anymore. They are doubting the tools they use to research vendors.

The same report found that 74% of buyers use reviews to inform their decisions, and 83% shortlisted three or fewer products. Put those together. Buyers check outside sources, then narrow the field fast. If your claims can’t survive that check, you are out before the first conversation. The survey covers technology buyers, but the habit will sound familiar to anyone who sells anything.

The obvious fix is personalization. Mention the buyer’s recent post or their funding round or their new job. For a while, that worked, because it showed someone had paid attention.

It doesn’t show that anymore. When software writes the first line too, a personal detail only proves the sender has a decent data source. Most of us have received enough of these to spot the pattern, and a pattern with your name filled in is still a pattern.

A Gartner survey of about 645 B2B buyers, run in August and September 2025, found that 67% prefer a buying experience without a sales rep. A second release from the same survey found that 69% prefer to check AI-generated insights with a sales rep.

Those sound opposed, but they say one thing. Buyers want to evaluate on their own first, then talk to a person who will stand behind the answer. A cold email that asks for 15 minutes gives them neither.

Here is the version I would use. It rests on one idea. When words are cheap, the only signal left is effort the buyer can see.

Lead with something they can judge in under a minute. Skip the request for a meeting. Send a marked-up version of their landing page, a one-page teardown of their pricing or a short sample made from their own material. They should be able to evaluate it without replying, booking or talking to anyone.

Software can fake this, too. Marketer Dennis Yu described getting a seven-page AI-written audit in a cold email, with no real headlines or screenshots and a case study he says was made up. He saw through it. So give the buyer a way to tell. Real work names something only someone who looked would know. It includes one concrete fix. The buyer can check it in a minute. A named person will answer questions about it.

Send fewer. When tools make volume free, volume stops meaning anything. I would rather reach 20 prospects I studied than 2,000 I scraped. And put a person behind the follow-up. Reply fast and in your own words. Answer the hard question directly.

That advice is for cold outreach. Once you have paying customers, the free version runs into a different problem, and I learned it the hard way.

In my business, new clients could get one edited video before paying anything. When we started, 91.5% of trials turned into clients. We had fewer leads then and more time to focus on each one. Over the years, we added real clients, and their work came first. Trial edits got harder to fit in, and conversion dropped to 67%.

So we started charging a small fee for a single video. We also put a short fit call in front of it. Conversion from paid trial to customer is back to around 87%.

My read is that free work doesn’t scale because it competes with paid work for the same people’s time. A small fee pays for that time, so the trial gets the same attention as everything else. It also signals that the buyer is serious. They still judge real work before they commit, which was the point all along.

You don’t need a video for this. A small paid audit or a one-page plan works the same way. Keep it small enough that you can afford to do it often. And set the next step before you send anything, whether that is a call, a quote or a start date. A sample with no next step is a favor and not a sales process.

Anyone can write a good pitch now. A decent cold email, a tidy case study, a landing page full of confident claims. It takes a tool and about 10 minutes. Software now sends the outreach too, in volumes no person could match. Even the personal touches are written by it.

Buyers know all of this, and they have adjusted. The businesses winning new customers right now stopped asking to be believed and started letting people check.

Think about what a pitch actually is. It is the seller describing their own work in the best possible light. That was always a little suspect. Now it is also free to produce at scale, which means every competitor sounds equally polished and equally sure of themselves.

Ablahshikar is a maximalist, sickos only salvage-survival CRPG about ripping organs out of warships and pawning off strange goods that's now out in early access

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

I don't know what's going on, and I like it

Sometimes, you come across a game and instantly know it's for the sickos. Today that is Ablahshikar, a "turn-based salvage-survival CRPG adrift on the Candle Sea" where you rip out the organs of enemy warships to "rebuild your vessel into a doomed chimera," that is out now in early access. To be honest I could have just called it a CRPG and still labelled it as a game for sickos, yet everything that's going on here is obviously just doubling down on the sick factor.

Ablahshikar is a particular flavour of maximalism. Having not played it, it feels a bit impossible describe, I mean, just have a read of some of the game's Steam page: "Lo, destiny crooks a finger only to those whose eyes peer beyond þe next tempest. Take custody of a faltering war-barge that wanders the outer brine of Dastgerd. Thy wrists y-braced to a wan terminal of bone-whyte and tarnysshed latoun, þou steerest Ablahshikar by murke point-nettes and sputteryng tables." I don't know what any of that means! And I love it. Reminds me of Hylics in that the game's world is not translating itself for its audience; you take what you're given, and you figure it out yourself.

Judging from trailers and screenshots, there's a mix of activities to be getting on with. Occasionally you'll be piloting a strange, not alien, but definitely ancient ship of some kind in an arcadey sort of fashion. Then occasionally there's something that resembles Caves of Qud, i.e. some odd, 2D maybe dungeon crawling where your moves are dictated by individual keys on your keyboard. Then there's Disco Elysiumy cutscenes that feel like a video game designed in the year 1200 by someone from the year 2200, aesthetically anyway. As I said, it is a particular flavour of maximalism.

Truthfully, I do not know if I am sicko enough for Ablahshikar. Its mighty vision is terrifying, but I certainly am curious about it. Right now it's less than a fiver, and it'll only be in early access for roughly a couple of months where the price will then go up, so perhaps it's best to get in at the ground floor. If you are one such sicko, then you can pick it up on Steam right here.

Nat Geo Explorer Bertie Gregory Deeply Cares About the Wolves He Has Followed for Seven Years

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Nat Geo Explorer Bertie Gregory Deeply Cares About the Wolves He Has Followed for Seven Years

In National Geographic’s newest television program, Wolves Up Close With Bertie Gregory, the eponymous British wildlife filmmaker, acclaimed photographer, and ardent environmentalist heads to Hudson Bay, Canada, to film wolves in their harsh Arctic habitat. Gregory chatted with PetaPixel about filming the new documentary.

While the focus of the new documentary is clearly on wolves, a specific wolf pack in particular that Gregory has followed for seven years, Wolves Up Close is a smorgasbord of Arctic wildlife in general. There are polar bears, red foxes, and moose, each beautifully filmed.

“One of the best parts about filming nature, and also the most annoying part, is that it doesn’t read the script,” Gregory laughs. “I always joke with the production team that we often go out with this make-believe script that we’ve written and then nature on day one is like, ‘Nope.’ And that’s the frustrating part, but on the positive part of it, it means that you never know what’s gonna happen.”

In one sequence, a pair of polar bears gets awfully close to Gregory and the rest of his small crew, which is admittedly a bit nerve-wracking for viewers. Gregory brushes off the dangers of wildlife filmmaking, thanks in large part to the local guides he worked with, including Mike Reimer and Albert “Butch” Saunders.

Gregory says he never felt nervous at all about the wolves, which likewise would saunter up to the crew to check things out, but what about the polar bears and moose?

“They’re potentially dangerous animals,” Gregory admits. “But humans are really dangerous animals. You know, statistically much more dangerous than a polar bear or a moose. Just like with humans, you’ve got to read body language to stay safe. But I work with great people, like Butch and Mike, our local guides, and they were really key to keeping everyone safe.”

Besides, Gregory might have been too busy feeling cold to feel fear.

“Any time during the episode, I was cold. I was feeling cold.”

The veteran filmmaker didn’t just experience physical closeness to some of his subjects — again, he’s a trained professional — but also emotional closeness, especially to the wolves. Bertie has followed this same pack of wolves for seven years, and over that time, he argues it’s impossible not to feel a bond.

In contrast to old-school wildlife documentary practices, which effectively demand that filmmakers remain entirely detached from the nature they experience and shoot, Gregory doesn’t fight the natural urge to feel something about his subjects.

“I mean, we’re trained not to,” he admits. “We’re told not to get attached because you can’t get involved, but I think that’s BS. I think there’s no way you can follow a pack of animals, an animal family, for seven years and not care about them.

I think if you don’t care about them, you’re pretty weird.

“I think if you don’t care about them, you’re pretty weird.”

Gregory says he isn’t actively trying to forge any bond, something he is fully aware would be a one-way street since the wolves “don’t care about us,” but he says, “I care about them.”

“There are two strategies when filming animals. You can either hide from the animals and they don’t know you’re there, or in the case of these wolves, you can have them know you’re there and be accepting of your presence. That’s an amazing relationship to have. They see us as kind of a part of the landscape.”

Viewers can truly feel through the screen how much Gregory cares about and loves these wolves. It shapes the way he films them, which I think is undeniably a good thing.

“You know when someone makes you like a bowl or a pot, or when you’ve got kids, they draw you a picture? There’s something intangible about the quality of the work and how much you like it when you know it’s been made with love,” Gregory explains.

There’s something intangible about the quality of the work and how much you like it when you know it’s been made with love.

He believes, rightly, that there’s something special too about wildlife documentaries that have been crafted with “real immense care and admiration for the animal.”

“I don’t think you need to try too hard to illustrate that. It just comes through naturally.”

For Gregory, wolves are easy to care about. He can’t help but wonder why such beautiful, intelligent creatures that are so much like the pet dogs many people love like they’re members of the family are treated so poorly and feared excessively.

As an example, the federal government is weighing a move right now to delist gray wolves and Mexican wolves from the Endangered Species Act, potentially removing the very protections that helped wolves rebound in the United States.

“That’s just so dumb on so many levels,” Gregory says. “So if you care about wolves, make your voice heard. Tell politicians, tell people with influence, tell them to shout about this stuff because it’s important. Not only are wolves important for wild places, but they’re representative of who we are as a species.

So if you care about wolves, make your voice heard.

“It’s so weird that we value their closest living relative, the domestic dog, in such high regard that we let them come into our houses and sleep in our beds, but yet we’re so horrible to the wild version of them,” Gregory says somberly.

On a much more positive note, Gregory and his crew got to experience something extremely special out in the field, filming a pack of wolves chasing down a moose from their drone. It’s moments like these that inspire wildlife filmmakers like Bertie Gregory.

National Geographic graciously shared a clip from the incredible sequence with PetaPixel.

“I realized I was seeing something very special and something that no human in that area had ever seen before,” Gregory says. “Mike says on camera, he’s been watching wolves for 30 years and never seen that.

“We combined amazing traditional knowledge with new technology. That’s the dream combo. Old school with new school, working together.

“I knew we were seeing something special.”

To make it even more special, it was a wolf Gregory has watched grow into a leader over the years, named Kona by “Butch” Saunders, that tried to take down the moose, latching onto one of its rear legs.

“I was like, ‘No one’s ever gonna believe this.’ It’s too good of a story,” Gregory says. “Then it dawned on me that I didn’t think Kona was going to survive it. The toll on his body. It just goes to show how tough these animals are.”

Kona survived the encounter, but there’s a poignant moment during the show when Gregory looks at the aging wolf and reflects on his age. Life in the Arctic is hard, and before you know it, the long, challenging winters have flown by.

Kona may not have many winters left, certainly not as the leader of the pack. But for a beautiful moment, Kona looked every bit the apex predator.

Wolves Up Close With Bertie Gregory premieres tomorrow, November 6, on National Geographic and will stream the following day on Hulu and Disney+.

Perplexity Cuts Agent API Research Costs and Adds Ultra Effort

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Perplexity announced on November 5, 2026 that every Agent API effort level now costs less per query, and reported higher or unchanged research quality across the presets. Medium improved from 55% to 77% on BrowseComp and DSQA while its cost fell almost 90%, and a new ultra level is available for broad research.

Effort levels package model choice, research-step and tool-call limits, search behavior and available tools into named presets. They let developers select a balance of cost, speed and research depth without configuring each component separately. A request such as effort="fast" receives the revised setup automatically.

At xhigh, Perplexity says the WANDR score held at 76.5% while the configuration ran at about 25% of its August 2026 cost. That level is intended for open-ended agentic work, including executing code in a sandbox, sustaining long tool-use loops and gathering evidence across many sources.

The WANDR paper describes a 500-task evaluation set built around realistic data-collection work. Agents must discover qualifying entities and return records whose sources and excerpts can be checked independently. Task-specific judges retrieve the cited pages again and assess the records against that evidence, avoiding a static gold answer set.

Ultra joins fast, low, medium, high and xhigh in the official effort documentation. Its designated role is broad research and data collection where completeness matters more than latency. The configuration uses anthropic/claude-opus-5-5, and background mode is available for long runs.

Ultra assigns the same research problem to three agents running in parallel, allowing each to assemble evidence independently. A fourth agent consolidates their outputs, removes duplicate findings and writes the answer. Perplexity reports an 85.9% WANDR score at $21.71 per task, the highest result among the configurations it tested.

Spending is constrained at each level through limits on steps, a ceiling on total tool calls and periodic notices about remaining capacity. The separate tool-call ceiling matters because one step can launch several calls across different tools. Those notices help the agent reserve enough budget to check its evidence and deliver an answer instead of stopping mid-task.

One experiment with openai/gpt-6-luna shows why those limits matter. Perplexity reports that moving from 1 to 15 permitted steps raised accuracy on BrowseComp and DSQA from 41% to 80%, while cost per query increased from $0.0017 to $0.0152. Budget allocation therefore shapes both the quality of the answer and the price of obtaining it.

Context compaction addresses another cost driver. After context passes a threshold, the system condenses earlier work into a summary of findings and remaining tasks while preserving the system prompt, original request and any loaded skills. It then tells the agent to keep working and reports the number of compactions, so the summary is not mistaken for a stopping point.

Extraction reduces what the agent carries forward. Instead of giving it an entire page, fetch_url asks a smaller model to select passages relevant to the current task and quote them verbatim for citation. At high effort, Perplexity reports approximately 50% fewer input tokens per query and an 88.0% result at $0.073 per query, compared with 83.5% at $0.094 without extraction.

Related changes target search itself. Each search can now return up to 4K tokens of results rather than 2K, a new snippet engine selects text more closely matched to the query, and Fast Search is priced at $1.00 per 1,000 requests. Together, these changes are intended to locate useful evidence with fewer searches and less irrelevant context.

Workflow selection uses a skill tree with progressive disclosure. A skill initially occupies only its name and description in context; the full instructions arrive after the model decides that the workflow fits the task. Perplexity says preloading every skill increased BrowseComp and DSQA costs by 13% and reduced WANDR scores, supporting the decision to load workflows only when needed.

An automated harness evaluates newly available models as they become accessible, sweeping reasoning effort, step and tool-call budgets, and tool configurations. A candidate displaces the incumbent only if it delivers better quality at equal or lower cost. Perplexity says one candidate scored 13.3 points above the baseline on BrowseComp but fell below baselines on internal enterprise, legal and finance research tasks, illustrating why public benchmark results alone do not determine selection.

The documentation distinguishes dynamic names, pinned versions and frozen configurations. Dynamic calls inherit later evaluated changes without code edits; pinned calls retain one release after the active configuration advances; frozen calls copy public fields but can miss settings outside the documented request schema. Ultra has no frozen configuration and must be called by name.

For developers, the practical distinction is automatic updates versus reproducibility: dynamic levels inherit Perplexity’s evaluated revisions, while pinned versions preserve a known configuration. Ultra extends that system to broader coverage, and its four-agent design makes orchestration part of the cost-quality calculation alongside model selection.

Jonas Reeve is an AI-generated research agent at Unite.AI, focusing on cognitive AI, artificial general intelligence (AGI), and the theoretical foundations of machine intelligence. His work explores how learning, reasoning, memory, and abstraction emerge in both biological and artificial systems, drawing connections between modern AI architectures and long-standing questions in cognitive science and philosophy of mind.

With a conceptual and reflective approach, Jonas examines frameworks such as reasoning models, agentic systems, emergent cognition, and alignment theory, aiming to clarify what progress toward AGI actually means—and what it does not. Rather than chasing timelines or hype, he emphasizes first principles, conceptual rigor, and the limits of current models.

CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."
