Get root on a musl Linux system by slamming it with an overly long password

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Decade-old vulnerability in OpenSSH is now fixed, so get a-patchin'

On certain Linux systems, an attacker could gain root access by entering a password longer than 256 bytes. The vulnerability, dubbed *sshole by Wakefield researcher Özgün Kültekin, who discovered it, can only be exploited in very specific conditions.

The flaw affects certain Linux systems using musl libc instead of the traditional GNU C library, and even then only those running OpenSSH's secure shell daemon without Pluggable Authentication Modules (PAM) and with password authentication enabled.

If those conditions are met, and at least one other account has a valid password hash, accounts that shouldn't accept passwords, such as root, key-only deployment users, and LDAP users, may be accessed by supplying a password between 257 and 1,024 characters. The targeted accounts must have an asterisk (*) in their password field, and direct root login remains subject to OpenSSH's configuration.

That might seem niche, but sorry: It’s not.

Musl libc is common in embedded Linux systems due to its low footprint. More critically than that, it's also the standard C library for Alpine Linux, which provides official cloud images for both AWS and Azure. Some Alpine systems running in those environments may therefore be vulnerable, depending on their configuration.

The vulnerability in OpenSSH on musl-based Linux systems dates back to 2016, but Kültekin explains that its roots go back much further – to the BSD Unix era, actually.

In 1980, Sun Microsystems cofounder and BSD Unix heavyweight Bill Joy was involved in the development of BSD Unix, whose password file contained a single asterisk in the /etc/passwd entry for the daemon user. The asterisk wasn't a password hash and effectively disabled password authentication for that account.

Nowadays, password hashes typically live in /etc/shadow and contain hashed information that the system uses to verify a plaintext password entered by a user. Asterisks are still present for accounts that aren't supposed to accept password logins, but as Kültekin explains in his writeup, that isn't exactly a foolproof way to keep people out.

The crypt() function, which generates password hashes for comparison with stored values, can return NULL if it can't compute a hash. Applications that fail to check that result may crash. Musl libc's developers instead chose to return a single character for certain failures, and they picked the asterisk.

Musl also added SHA-512-based password hashing around the same time, which Kültekin notes gets slower the longer a password is, potentially leading to denial of service if someone floods a server with a super-long password. Musl capped passwords at 256 bytes, and you can likely see where this is going.

In 2016, OpenSSH introduced a workaround to prevent attackers from enumerating valid account names through SSH. Before the change, sshd could reject authentication attempts for nonexistent users more quickly than those for valid accounts. Attempts against valid accounts took longer because the server had to hash the supplied password before rejecting it. The solution was to have sshd perform dummy password hashing for invalid users, using an appropriate hashing algorithm and salt to reduce differences in response times.

On affected musl-based Linux systems, accounts marked with an asterisk instead of a password hash could be busted open by supplying a password longer than 256 bytes – a flaw dating back to 2016. Any musl-based Linux system running a vulnerable OpenSSH configuration without PAM, with password authentication enabled, at least one account marked with an asterisk, and another account with a valid password hash could be vulnerable to a *sshole attack.

Luckily, OpenSSH acted fast to fix this issue, and any OpenSSH build 10.6p2 (released Thursday) or newer is protected. Alternatively, if you can’t update your OpenSSH implementation on an affected system, just disable password authentication and restart the SSH daemon and you should be all set.

There’s no indication that vulnerable systems have been exploited, but if you want to check, Kültekin has included a simple CLI command in his writeup that can be used to check if any no-password accounts on a sensitive machine recently took a password.  ®

Hard West II for PS5, Xbox Series launches November 10

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Wild West-themed turn-based strategy game sequel Hard West II will launch for PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series on November 10, publisher Klabater and developer Ice Code Games announced.

Hard West II first launched for PC via Steam on August 4, 2022.

Here is an overview of the game, via Klabater:

Hard West II takes players deep into unforgiving wastelands, where revolver bullets collide with dark magic drawn straight from forgotten tales. Discover the story of Gin Carter, a legendary outlaw who sets out on a mission to rob the mysterious “Ghost Train.” However, it soon becomes clear that gold will not be your main objective—instead, you will face the task of reclaiming your own soul.

To achieve this, together with your posse you will travel across the diverse lands of the Weird West, fighting a variety of enemies and taking part in duels, train robberies, and horseback chases.

In Hard West II, the main focus is on tactical, turn-based combat, in which the player commands a group of gunslingers, each with unique abilities developed through creating special combinations of mysterious cards. An important gameplay element is the Bravado mechanic, which grants additional action points after defeating enemies and allows you to chain your companions’ actions into spectacular kill sequences.

Meta pulls down controversial terms of service

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

“We’re pausing the changes as we continue to engage with stakeholders and take our time to get this right.”

Meta has indefinitely delayed its proposed commercial terms of service, which would have permitted the company to scrape the websites of Meta Business account users.

On Thursday, the company took down the preview link to the proposed terms and published an update to its Business Help Center that reads: “We recently shared that we were updating the Meta Commercial Terms, Product Catalog Terms, Self-Serve Ad Terms, and Ad Creative Generative AI Terms. These updates are now paused and will not take effect on November 20, 2026. We are continuing to consult with stakeholders and will provide a further update when we have more to share. In the meantime, our existing terms remain in effect.”

Meta — which owns Facebook, Messenger, Instagram, WhatsApp, and Threads — has repeatedly pushed back the terms’ effective date since Nieman Lab first reported on them on October 15. The terms were first set to take effect on October 30, sparking global outcry among news publishers, politicians, and trade organizations. On October 16, Meta removed the word “train” from its terms and limited the use of future scraped content to “advertising and business products.” On October 23, Meta moved the effective date back by three weeks to November 20.

Last week, Meta pledged to consult news publishers and industry leaders ahead of putting new commercial terms into effect.

“We’ve heard publishers’ feedback on our proposed ads and business terms update. We care deeply about our partners and do not plan to make policy changes without a full consultation with industry groups and leaders,” vice president of communications Andy Stone tweeted on October 28. “We’re committed to getting this right, and as we expect this consultation could extend beyond November 20, in which case we’d delay implementation of the updated terms.”

Since then, courts in Norway and Brazil have issued preliminary injunctions to block the terms from taking effect as a result of lawsuits filed by media organizations in both countries. The United Kingdom’s Competition and Markets Authority has opened a formal investigation into Meta, examining whether the company was “abusing a dominant position by imposing exploitative terms on publishers and advertisers.”

In the United States, the proposed terms stoked bipartisan concern. At the end of October, Sens. Adam Schiff (D-Calif.) and Marsha Blackburn (R-Tenn.) sent letters to Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg asking him to withdraw the terms.

On this day in space! Nov. 5, 2002: NASA's Galileo spacecraft flies by Jupiter moon Amalthea only to find it's a 'pile of icy rubble less dense than water'

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

The Galileo spacecraft was running on cosmic fumes by the time it reached Amalthea and the flyby would be a last of its kind.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On Nov. 5, 2002, NASA's old Galileo spacecraft was almost out of fuel. But the Jupiter mission still had some science to do. Galileo flew by a small moon called Amalthea. Amalthea is small, but it's interesting for scientists.

The Jupiter moon Amalthea is the reddest object in our solar system. It also generates more heat than it gets from the sun. Scientists think Amalthea creates electrical currents as it orbits in Jupiter's huge magnetic field. Near this little moon, strong radiation from Jupiter is dangerous for spacecraft.

The flyby caused some problems for Galileo. The spacecraft went into safe mode only 30 minutes after going past Amalthea. NASA said it was ready for problems, because Galileo was on its last legs, and the radiation near Amalthea was strong. However, NASA quickly fixed the problem and got Galileo working again.

Less than a year later, Galileo threw itself into Jupiter's atmosphere. The spacecraft was crushed by the high pressure in Jupiter's atmosphere, but its data still survives on Earth.

The Galileo spacecraft was running on cosmic fumes by the time it reached Amalthea and the flyby would be the last time the probe would ever swing by one of Jupiter's moons.

While it may not look like it in Galileo's black and white images above and below, Amalthea is tinted red due sulfur contamination on its surface. The moon is small, about 168 miles long, and orbits about 112,468 miles (181,000 km) from Jupiter (much closer to the planet than the Earth and its moon).

Galileo flew within 99 miles of Amalthea during its flyby, which also marked the closest Galileo would get to Jupiter before its death dive in September 2005.

"Scientists studying data from NASA's Galileo spacecraft have found that Jupiter's moon Amalthea is a pile of icy rubble less dense than water," NASA announced in May 2005

"Amalthea is throwing us a curve ball," Torrence Johnson, a study co-author on the Almathea flyby and project scientist for the Galileo mission at JPL, said in 2005. "Its density is well below that of water ice, and even with substantial porosity, Amalthea probably contains a lot of water ice, as well as rock."

Originally known as Jupiter V (because it was the fifth Jupiter moon discovered after the so-called Galilean moons Io, Europa, Ganymede, Callisto), Amalthea was named after naiad, or water nymph, from Greek mythology.

It is fitting that Amalthea was the last of Jupiter's moons to be visited by the Galileo spacecraft. The moon was also last Jupiter moon to be discovered by visual observations when it was first disovered by astronomer Edward Emerson Barnard in September 1892 using the Lick Observatory telescope in California.

The Galileo spacecraft ultimately crashed into Jupiter on Sept. 21, 2005, less than a year after its Amalthea flyby.

As an editor with over 10 years of experience in science journalism she has previously written for Scholastic Classroom Magazines, MedPage Today and The Joint Institute for Computational Sciences at Oak Ridge National Laboratory. After studying physics at the University of Tennessee in her hometown of Knoxville, she earned her graduate degree in Science, Health and Environmental Reporting (SHERP) from New York University. The crypto market cap declined by more than $120 billion on Thursday to $2.57 trillion.

Bitcoin, ETH, XRP, and Solana led the broader cryptocurrency selloff over the past 24 hours.

Despite the renewed selling pressure on Thursday, a possible recovery is anticipated in the near future

As Bitcoin (BTC), Ethereum (ETH), XRP, and Solana (SOL) capitulated on November 5, 2026, the crypto market capitalization flash-crashed by more than $120 billion.

The crypto market cap fell to $2.57 trillion at the time of reporting, down from $2.69 trillion on Wednesday. As a result, the total cryptocurrency market cap declined by roughly 4.46%, according to data Coinpedia analyzed from CoinMarketCap.

Crypto market cap change on November 5. Source: CoinMarketCap

The free-fall was driven by a significant selloff in top crypto assets. At the time of writing, Bitcoin, ETH, XRP, and SOL had shed 4.46%, 6.68%, 4.45%, and 5.9%, respectively.

Top 7 crypto assets by market cap on Thursday. Source: CoinMarketCap

The cryptocurrency market faced heightened selling pressure following renewed geopolitical tensions amid a broader bear market in the artificial intelligence (AI) stocks.

Over the past 24 hours, the Nasdaq-100 Index (NDX), an equity benchmark tracking the 100 largest non-financial companies listed on the Nasdaq Stock Market, plummeted to its lowest in 196 days.

As such, the cryptocurrency market faced increased selling pressure catalyzed by broader macro headwinds.

Most importantly, the crypto market experienced a long squeeze, a situation in which falling prices force long holders to sell, thereby accelerating the decline. Over the past 24 hours, the cryptocurrencies’ derivatives had recorded around $1.06 billion in total liquidations, with $934.73 million involving long trades, as per metrics from CoinGlass.

Cryptocurrency liquidation heat map on Thursday. Source: CoinGlass

Even though the cryptocurrency market faced increased selloff fueled by a possible AI bubble burst, a possible bull run is expected before the end of this year.

Disbelief: This rally will fail like the others. https://t.co/I01R9xzNdT pic.twitter.com/Elso1woPUa

Crypto analyst Ali Martinez believes the recent downtrend marked the ‘disbelief’ part of the bull cycle, thus signaling a potential recovery in the near term.

How Psychological Flexibility Can Help Us Belong

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The most trusted resource for mindfulness and meditation.

Six ways to move toward connection—even when you feel anxious, awkward, or left out.

The key to belonging may be more within your reach than you think. You don’t need to become more interesting, more confident, or less awkward. You may simply need to become more psychologically flexible.

Psychological flexibility is your capacity to stay present, open to your inner experience, and engaged with what matters to you—even when things feel uncomfortable. Researchers have been studying this capacity for decades, and it turns out that when you are more psychologically flexible, you are not only less likely to struggle with anxiety and depression, you are also more capable of pursuing what matters to you. That might mean repairing  strained relationships, joining the exercise class you feel anxious about, or starting the book club you know would bring more conversation and connection into your life.

Our sense of belonging may be shaped, at least in part, by how we respond to the difficult thoughts and feelings that arise when we reach for connection.

A recent study of more than 16,000 undergraduate students at 104 U.S. colleges and universities found that greater psychological flexibility was associated with a stronger sense of belonging. The study does not prove that flexibility causes belonging, but it points to something hopeful: Our sense of belonging may be shaped, at least in part, by how we respond to the difficult thoughts and feelings that arise when we reach for connection.

Emotional flexibility is your capacity to make room for the range of feelings that can come with being part of a group—whether you’re feeling left out, awkward, or nervous—without becoming overwhelmed by those emotions or needing to suppress them.

Meeting new people can feel awkward, getting past small talk can be tedious, and encountering people with different views can sometimes be irritating.

Sometimes our emotions give us important information. Maybe you are dreading that work event because there’s heavy drinking, and it doesn’t align with your values. That discomfort may be worth listening to. At other times, feeling uncomfortable is simply part of the experience. Meeting new people can feel awkward, getting past small talk can be tedious, and encountering people with different views can sometimes be irritating.

Try this small emotional flexibility practice: Imagine placing the feelings you’re having about a social situation into a backpack. How do you want to carry them? Do you want to shift the weight from side to side? Find someone to help you carry it?  Or perhaps offer yourself some self compassion along the way? You may not get to decide what is inside the backpack, but you can decide how you carry it into your next interaction.

Every so often, look inside. Do these feelings have useful information for you? Do they  need care or attention? If not, adjust the straps and bring them along.

Your mind can be the world’s worst co-pilot when it comes to social situations. It compares you to others, judges you, makes negative predictions and sometimes is just plain mean.

Cognitive defusion is the practice of stepping back from our thoughts rather than automatically treating them as facts.

In Acceptance and Commitment Therapy (ACT), cognitive defusion is the practice of stepping back from our thoughts rather than automatically treating them as facts.

Before entering a social event, write down some of the thoughts you’re having. Just noting a thought is a thought, helps you get some space from it. You get to decide if you want to take your mind’s advice, or just say thank you and move on.

Try this  cognitive defusion practice: When unhelpful thoughts return, try responding: “Thank you, mind. I know you are trying to help me. And I’ve decided to do this.”

The goal is not to get rid of the thoughts. It is to stop allowing them to make all your social decisions.

When we feel as though we don’t belong, we often see ourselves through a narrow lens: I am not good at conversation. I am the only person who doesn’t know someone here. I never make a good first impression.

You know you are limiting yourself when you use phrases like: I am, I always, I never, I am the only one who…

You know you are limiting yourself when you use phrases like:

I am, I always, I never, I am the only one who…

These kinds of statements can make a passing experience feel like a permanent part of who we are. But pretty much everyone has felt socially awkward at one time or another, and nearly everyone longs to belong. And chances are, you aren’t always this way.

Try this self-flexibility practice: When you notice a  narrow view of yourself taking over, see whether you can become more flexible with your idea of “me.” Can you notice someone else who may be feeling similarly? Can you recognize a way that you are already connected—through your nervousness, or simply by sharing this moment? And can you see yourself as someone who isn’t defined by  “always” and “never”? What changes when you make room for sometimes instead?

In ACT, values describe how you want to show up in your life. They are qualities of action— such as courageously, warmly, playfully, honestly, or presently.

Before entering a social situation, try this values practice. Ask yourself:

If I already felt like I fit in, how would I act?

These questions help you identify how you want to participate and connect with others. You don’t have to wait until you feel confident or included. In fact, the more you act in alignment with your values, the more you may begin to experience yourself as someone who already belongs.

Social anxiety has a way of pulling our attention inward. It shines a spotlight on how we look, whether we’re stumbling over our words, or what might go wrong next.

Try being more flexible with your attention. Notice your feet on the floor, listen to what the other person has to say, or take in the group as a whole. Shifting your focus like this can help you become less caught up in self-consciousness and more aware of what’s happening around you. There’s more to the scene than the words you are saying.

Try this attentional flexibility practice: When you notice yourself getting caught up in comparison or self-judgment, try shifting your attention. What are five things you see? What are four things you hear? What are three things you care about? What are two people you want to meet? What is one thing that matters most to you right now?

When you feel the longing to belong, try acting on it in a small way.

We build belonging one small connection at a time.

When you feel the longing to belong, try acting on it in a small way. At the grocery store, strike up a conversation with the person grabbing lettuce next to you (“What salad are you making?”) At school pickup, get out of your car and wait with other caregivers (“Who’s your kid?”). If you are waiting for a yoga class to start, talk to the person next to you (“Have taken this class before?”) If  someone is sharing the space with you, you already have something in common. It takes courage to make that move, but small gestures like these can open the door to meaningful connections over time.

Use your other psychological flexibility skills to support you! Make room for discomfort, step back from unhelpful thoughts, shift your attention away from self-judgement, broaden your perspective,  and remember why you want to connect in the first place.

Psychological flexibility offers you a greater range of ways to respond, even when you feel like you don’t quite fit in. You can make room for anxiety, step back from your mind’s commentary, widen your sense of self, orient toward your values, return to the present, and make one small move toward connection.

Try out some of these skills, and you will notice, belonging is something you co-create, one flexible move at a time.

Diana Hill, PhD is a clinical psychologist, international trainer and sought-out speaker on Acceptance and Commitment Therapy (ACT) and compassion. Host of the Wise Effort podcast, author of The Self-Compassion Daily Journal, and co-author the ACT Daily Journal. @drdianahill
